g^{\prime}_{k}\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathbf{C}}\begin{pmatrix}f_{k}\\
g_{k}\end{pmatrix}
. In particular,
f_{k}=0
means
that
M(x_{k})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\ast&\ast\end{pmatrix}
and
g_{k}=0

means that
M(x_{k})=\begin{pmatrix}\ast&\ast\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
.

Lemma 5.3

(i) One cannot have
f_{1}g_{2}=f_{2}g_{1}=0
.

(ii) The ratio
(f_{1}g_{2}:f_{2}g_{1})\in{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
is well defined from
M
,
independently of the particular choices of non-zero columns.

(iii) This ratio is invariant by the group action of diagonal matrices

M\mapsto\Gamma M\Delta^{-1}
,
\Gamma,\Delta\in{\text{D}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})}
.

Proof. -
(i) We first prove that one cannot have
f_{1}=f_{2}=0
nor
g_{1}=g_{2}=0
.
We prove only the first impossibility, the second one is similar.
So assume that
f_{1}=f_{2}=0
. Then
M(x_{1})
and
M(x_{2})
have the form

\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\ast&\ast\end{pmatrix}
,
i.e.
both
m_{1,1}

and
m_{1,2}
vanish at
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. Since
\det M\neq 0
, they cannot
both be null, say
m_{1,j}\neq 0
. Then by
2.4
,
the fact that
\sigma_{q}m_{1,j}=(\rho_{1}/\sigma_{j})x^{-2}m_{1,j}
implies that

x_{1}x_{2}\equiv\rho_{1}/\sigma_{j}
, contradicting (NS).

Now to the point: assume for instance that
f_{1}=0
(the case
g_{2}=0

being similar). Since by the previous statement
f_{2}\neq 0
, the assumption

f_{2}g_{1}=0
would entail
g_{1}=0
, contradicting the definition of

\begin{pmatrix}f_{k}\\
g_{k}\end{pmatrix}
as a non zero column.

(ii) If for instance
\begin{pmatrix}f^{\prime}_{1}\\
g^{\prime}_{1}\end{pmatrix}\neq 0
, then

\begin{pmatrix}f^{\prime}_{1}\\
g^{\prime}_{1}\end{pmatrix}=\lambda\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}\\
g_{1}\end{pmatrix}
with
\lambda\neq 0
, whence

(f^{\prime}_{1}g_{2},f_{2}g^{\prime}_{1})=\lambda(f_{1}g_{2},f_{2}g_{1})
, so that

(f^{\prime}_{1}g_{2}:f_{2}g^{\prime}_{1})=(f_{1}g_{2}:f_{2}g_{1})
.

(iii) Let
M^{\prime}:=\Gamma M\Delta^{-1}
and let
j_{1},j_{2}
the indexes of the selected
nonzero columns. With obvious notations,
f^{\prime}_{i}=\dfrac{\gamma_{1}}{\delta_{j_{i}}}f_{i}

and
g^{\prime}_{i}=\dfrac{\gamma_{2}}{\delta_{j_{i}}}g_{i}
, whence

f^{\prime}_{1}g^{\prime}_{2}=\dfrac{\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}{\delta_{j_{1}}\delta_{j_{2}}}f_{1}g_{2}
and

f^{\prime}_{2}g^{\prime}_{1}=\dfrac{\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}{\delta_{j_{1}}\delta_{j_{2}}}f_{2}g_{1}
.
The conclusion follows.

\Box

We thus obtain a well defined map
41
41

41

This map
is obviously related to our selection of the pair
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
and it should
properly be denoted
\Pi_{1,2}
but we don’t need to do that for the moment; see
section
7
.

\Pi:F\rightarrow{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})},

which goes to the quotient as

\Pi:{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}

(the shorthand notations
F:=F_{R,S,{\underline{x}}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{F}}_{R,S,{\underline{x}}}
were introduced
in part
4.1.1
of subsection
4.1
).
These maps (denoted by the same letter, which, hopefully, will cause no confusion)
will come back in full glory when we describe
F
and
{\mathcal{F}}
as fibered spaces.

We leave to the reader the proof of the following, which gives in generic
cases a necessary and sufficient condition for two ordered pairs to give
the same point in
{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
.

Lemma 5.4

Let
f_{1},f_{2},g_{1},g_{2}\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
and
p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
. Then:

\left\{\dfrac{p_{1}q_{2}}{p_{2}q_{1}}=\dfrac{f_{1}g_{2}}{f_{2}g_{1}}\right\}\Longleftrightarrow\left\{\exists\ \lambda,\mu\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\;:\;\begin{pmatrix}f_{i}\\
g_{i}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&\mu\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}p_{i}\\
q_{i}\end{pmatrix},i=1,2\right\}.

\Box

5.1.2
Two special fibers of the projective invariant
\Pi

The fibers
42
42

42

We shall find in
6.3
that there
are two more special fibers.

\Pi^{-1}(0),\Pi^{-1}(\infty)\subset F
have special
significance. For example, they contain all reducible matrices. Indeed, if
M\in F

is reducible,
i.e.
(definition
4.1
) if it corresponds
to a reducible system in
E
, then one of its coefficients vanishes (
cf.

4.1.2
) and
M(x_{1})
,
M(x_{2})
acquire one of
the forms
\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\ast&\ast\end{pmatrix}
,

\begin{pmatrix}\ast&\ast\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
. Then
f_{1}f_{2}g_{1}g_{2}=0
,
so that
\Pi(M)=0
or
\Pi(M)=\infty
.

However, the converse is not true. We briefly describe irreducible matrices
M

in
\Pi^{-1}(0)\cup\Pi^{-1}(\infty)
. This means that
f_{1}f_{2}g_{1}g_{2}=0
:

\Pi^{-1}(0)=(f_{1}=0)\cup(g_{2}=0)\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\Pi^{-1}(\infty)=(f_{2}=0)\cup(g_{1}=0).

We treat the case
f_{1}=0
, the other ones being entirely similar. To simplify
the discussion, we shall assume that
F
contains no reducible matrices,
i.e.
that there is no splitting of (FR) (theorem
4.3
).
A more general situation is tackled by other means in section

6.3
.

If
f_{1}=0
, then
M(x_{1})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\ast&\ast\end{pmatrix}
.
Since by assumption of irreducibility
m_{1,1},m_{1,2}\neq 0
, we see that
these coefficients have the forms
m_{1,1}=c_{1}{\theta_{q}}(-x/x_{1}){\theta_{q}}(-x/x^{\prime}_{1})
,

m_{1,2}=c_{2}{\theta_{q}}(-x/x_{1}){\theta_{q}}(-x/x^{\prime\prime}_{1})
, with arbitrary
c_{1},c_{2}\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}

and
x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime\prime}_{1}\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
such that
x_{1}x^{\prime}_{1}=\rho_{1}/\sigma_{1}
,

x_{1}x^{\prime\prime}_{1}=\rho_{1}/\sigma_{2}
.

Up to the action
M\mapsto\Gamma M\Delta^{-1}
by diagonal matrices

\Gamma:={\text{Diag}}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
,
\Delta:={\text{Diag}}(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
,
we may and shall take
c_{1}=c_{2}=1
. Then
m_{2,1}\in V_{2,\frac{\rho_{2}}{\sigma_{1}}}

and
m_{2,2}\in V_{2,\frac{\rho_{2}}{\sigma_{2}}}
are arbitrary non zero elements,
except for the conditions on
\det M
(vanishing at
{\underline{x}}
but not everywhere).
The remaining gauge freedom (while retaining the form
c_{1}=c_{2}=1
) requires

\gamma_{1}=\delta_{1}=\delta_{2}
,
i.e.
only
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
-action on
(m_{2,1},m_{2,2})

is allowed. So we shall identify the image of
f_{1}=0
in
{\mathcal{F}}
to a subset
of
\dfrac{V_{2,\frac{\rho_{2}}{\sigma_{1}}}\times V_{2,\frac{\rho_{2}}{\sigma_{2}}}}{{\mathbf{C}}^{*}}\cdot

Non triviality of
\det M
.

We have equivalences:

\displaystyle\det M=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow m_{2,1}{\theta_{q}}(-x/x_{1}){\theta_{q}}(-x/x^{\prime\prime}_{1})=m_{2,2}{\theta_{q}}(-x/x_{1}){\theta_{q}}(-x/x^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow m_{2,1}{\theta_{q}}(-x/x^{\prime\prime}_{1})=m_{2,2}{\theta_{q}}(-x/x^{\prime}_{1}).

Since
x^{\prime}_{1}\not\equiv x^{\prime\prime}_{1}
(this follows from (NR)), we conclude
that
m_{2,1}=c{\theta_{q}}(-x/x^{\prime}_{1}){\theta_{q}}(-x/x^{\prime}_{2})
and

m_{2,2}=d{\theta_{q}}(-x/x^{\prime\prime}_{1}){\theta_{q}}(-x/x^{\prime\prime}_{2})
, where
c,d\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
and

x^{\prime}_{1}x^{\prime}_{2}=\rho_{2}/\sigma_{1}
,
x^{\prime}_{1}x^{\prime\prime}_{2}=\rho_{2}/\sigma_{2}
. Actually,

x^{\prime}_{2}=x_{1}\rho_{2}/\rho_{1}=x^{\prime\prime}_{2}
; and
\det M=0\Leftrightarrow c=d
.
So we see that matrices
M\in(f_{1}=0)
such that
\det M=0
correspond
to a
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
-line in
V_{2,\frac{\rho_{2}}{\sigma_{1}}}\times V_{2,\frac{\rho_{2}}{\sigma_{2}}}

and their classes in
{\mathcal{F}}
to a unique point in the associated projective space.

Vanishing of
\det M
at
{\underline{x}}
.

Vanishing of
\det M
at
x_{1}
is ensured by the above chosen form. Vanishing
at
x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}
then amounts to
two
linear conditions (this is because

\sigma_{q}(\det M)/\det M=x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}x_{4}
):

\begin{cases}m_{2,1}(x_{2}){\theta_{q}}(-x_{2}/x_{1}){\theta_{q}}(-x_{2}/x^{\prime\prime}_{1})=m_{2,2}(x_{2}){\theta_{q}}(-x_{2}/x_{1}){\theta_{q}}(-x_{2}/x^{\prime}_{1}),\\
m_{2,1}(x_{3}){\theta_{q}}(-x_{3}/x_{1}){\theta_{q}}(-x_{3}/x^{\prime\prime}_{1})=m_{2,2}(x_{3}){\theta_{q}}(-x_{3}/x_{1}){\theta_{q}}(-x_{3}/x^{\prime}_{1}).\end{cases}

Simplifications are allowed by (NR) and yield:

\begin{cases}m_{2,1}(x_{2}){\theta_{q}}(-x_{2}/x^{\prime\prime}_{1})=m_{2,2}(x_{2}){\theta_{q}}(-x_{2}/x^{\prime}_{1}),\\
m_{2,1}(x_{3}){\theta_{q}}(-x_{3}/x^{\prime\prime}_{1})=m_{2,2}(x_{3}){\theta_{q}}(-x_{3}/x^{\prime}_{1}).\end{cases}

Each of the two linear conditions is non trivial (because, again by (NR),

x^{\prime}_{1}\not\equiv x^{\prime\prime}_{1}
), whence defines a hyperplane in the

4
-dimensional product space

V_{2,\frac{\rho_{2}}{\sigma_{1}}}\times V_{2,\frac{\rho_{2}}{\sigma_{2}}}
. These
hyperplanes are distinct: indeed, using non splitting, one can find

f\in V_{2,\frac{\rho_{2}}{\sigma_{1}}}
and
g\in V_{2,\frac{\rho_{2}}{\sigma_{2}}}
,
each of them vanishing at
x_{2}
but not at
x_{3}
; then, for a proper
choice of
c,d\in{\mathbf{C}}^{2}
, the pair
(m_{2,1},m_{2,2}):=(cf,dg)
belongs
to the first hyperplane but not to the second one. Therefore their intersection
is a plane containing the line
\det M=0
. Going to the associated projective
space yields a line. So we conclude that the image of the component
(f_{1}=0)

is a projective line minus a point,
i.e.
an
affine
line
{\mathbf{C}}
.

The fiber
\Pi^{-1}(0)
has the two components
f_{1}=0
and
g_{2}=0
each
isomorphic to the line
{\mathbf{C}}
(this meaning that the obvious bijections are
biregular in the algebro-geometric sense). Matrices in the intersection of
these components have the form:

M=\begin{pmatrix}c_{1,1}{\theta_{q}}(-x/x_{1}){\theta_{q}}(-x/x^{\prime}_{1})&c_{1,2}{\theta_{q}}(-x/x_{1}){\theta_{q}}(-x/x^{\prime\prime}_{1})\\
c_{2,1}{\theta_{q}}(-x/x_{2}){\theta_{q}}(-x/x^{\prime}_{2})&c_{2,2}{\theta_{q}}(-x/x_{2}){\theta_{q}}(-x/x^{\prime\prime}_{2})\end{pmatrix}

for some arbitrary
c_{i,j}\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
and
x^{\prime}_{i},x^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
determined by
obvious conditions. The
\Gamma,\Delta
-action allows one to take three of
the
c_{i,j}
with value
1
and the fourth is then determined by the vanishing
of
\det M
at
x_{3}
, so our two projective lines intersect at exacly one point,
corresponding to the double degeneracy
f_{1}=g_{2}=0
.

Theorem 5.5

Assume non splitting of (FR) (
i.e.
all classes in
{\mathcal{F}}
are irreducible). The
special fibers
\Pi^{-1}(0)
and
\Pi^{-1}(\infty)
of
\Pi:{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}

are both made of two affine lines intersecting at one point.

\Box

The study of the other fibers will be done in section

6
.

5.1.3
An elliptic function

Along with the projective invariant
\Pi
, a central role will be played by the
following auxiliary function
43
43

43

This should really be denoted
\Phi_{1,2}
,
see footnote
41
page
41
.
:

\Phi(\xi):=\dfrac{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{x_{1}}{\rho_{1}}\xi\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{x_{2}}{\rho_{2}}\xi\right)}{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{x_{1}}{\rho_{2}}\xi\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{x_{2}}{\rho_{1}}\xi\right)}\cdot

It is readily verified that
\Phi
is an elliptic function on
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
,
i.e.
that

\Phi(q\xi)=\Phi(\xi)
, so, after the conventions of subsection

2.2
, we may and will see it as a mapping (denoted the
same)
\Phi:{\mathbf{E}_{q}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})
.

Under assumption (NR) there is no cancellation of zeroes between the numerator
and denominator (which are both holomorphic over
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
), so the elliptic function

\Phi
has degree
2
. Also:

(5.5.1)

\forall\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\;,\;\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\equiv\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}\Longrightarrow\Phi(\xi_{1})=\Phi(\xi_{2}).

(Argument: by ellipticity of
\Phi
, one may assume equality in the premise; and
then direct calculation yields the result.) More precisely, we get:

Proposition 5.6

(i) The elliptic function
\Phi
realizes a degree
2
ramified covering

{\mathbf{E}_{q}}\rightarrow{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
with
4
critical values.

(ii) Generic fibers have the form
\{\overline{\xi_{1}},\overline{\xi_{1}}\}
,
where
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}_{q}}
,

\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\equiv\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}
,

\xi_{1}\not\equiv\xi_{2}
.

(iii) The four singular fibers have the form
\{\overline{\xi}\}
, where

\xi\in{\mathcal{C}_{q}}
,
\xi^{2}\equiv\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}
.

\Box

Also note that the divisor of
\Phi
depends only on the local data:

{\text{div}}_{\mathbf{E}_{q}}(\Phi)=\left[\pi\left(-\frac{\rho_{1}}{x_{1}}\right)\right]+\left[\pi\left(-\frac{\rho_{2}}{x_{2}}\right)\right]-\left[\pi\left(-\frac{\rho_{1}}{x_{2}}\right)\right]-\left[\pi\left(-\frac{\rho_{2}}{x_{1}}\right)\right].

(Recall that
\pi:{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\rightarrow{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
is the canonical projection.)
Up to a non zero constant,
\Phi
is determined by this divisor.

5.1.4
An involution of
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}

Here is the geometrical meaning of
\Phi
. The relation

\xi_{1}\leftrightarrow\xi_{2}
whenever

\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\equiv\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}
defines an
involution on
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
, and the mapping
\Phi:{\mathbf{E}_{q}}\rightarrow{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
defines
a quotient of
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
by this involution. Up to a dilatation in
{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
, this
is the only realisation of this quotient with the following special fibers:

\displaystyle\text{Fiber at\penalty\ }0

\displaystyle=\left\{\pi\left(-\frac{\rho_{1}}{x_{1}}\right),\pi\left(-\frac{\rho_{2}}{x_{2}}\right)\right\},

\displaystyle\text{Fiber at\penalty\ }\infty

\displaystyle=\left\{\pi\left(-\frac{\rho_{1}}{x_{2}}\right),\pi\left(-\frac{\rho_{2}}{x_{1}}\right)\right\}.

5.2
Definition, gauge freedom

Definition 5.7

A
Mano decomposition
M=PQ
with factor
C\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})
is given by

P,Q\in{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathcal{O}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})})
such that
\sigma_{q}P=RP(Cx)^{-1}
and
\det P
vanishes
at
x_{1},x_{2}
. (It is understood that
x_{1},x_{2}
have previously been chosen and
we do not mention them in the terminology.)

It follows immediately from the definition, the assumptions on
M
and the
properties recalled in
2.4
that:

—

\det P\neq 0
and
\det P
vanishes at
[x_{1},x_{2};q]
with simple zeroes
and nowhere else,

—

\det Q\neq 0
and
\det Q
vanishes at
[x_{3},x_{4};q]
with simple zeroes
and nowhere else,

—

\sigma_{q}Q=CQ(Sx)^{-1}
,

—

\dfrac{\sigma_{q}(\det P)}{\det P}=\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{(\det C)x^{2}}
,

—

\dfrac{\sigma_{q}(\det Q)}{\det Q}=\dfrac{\det C}{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}x^{2}}
,

—

\det C\equiv(\rho_{1}\rho_{2})/(x_{1}x_{2})
.

5.2.1
First consequences

Since we expect
\det P
to have simple zeroes at
x_{1},x_{2}
, we can,
for
i=1,2
, choose a non zero column
\begin{pmatrix}p_{i}\\
q_{i}\end{pmatrix}

of
P(x_{i})
; and the other column
\begin{pmatrix}p^{\prime}_{i}\\
q^{\prime}_{i}\end{pmatrix}
then
necessarily belongs to
{\mathbf{C}}\begin{pmatrix}p_{i}\\
q_{i}\end{pmatrix}
. Arguments
similar to those used in the proof of lemma
5.3
yield the
following.

Lemma 5.8

(i) One cannot have
p_{1}q_{2}=p_{2}q_{1}=0
.

(ii) The ratio
(p_{1}q_{2}:p_{2}q_{1})\in{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
is well defined from
P
,
independently of the particular choices of non-zero columns.

\Box

The “invariant”
(p_{1}q_{2}:p_{2}q_{1})
will be related to
\Pi(M)
in proposition

5.16
and to the values of the elliptic function
\Phi

in lemma
5.12
.

5.2.2
Gauge freedom

Proposition 5.9

(Gauge freedom)

Let
M=PQ
a Mano decomposition with factor
C
.

(i) Let
\Lambda\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathcal{O}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})})
be such that
\Lambda[C]\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})
.
Let
P^{\prime}:=P\Lambda^{-1}
,
Q^{\prime}:=\Lambda Q
and
C^{\prime}:=\Lambda[C]
. Then

M=P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}
is a Mano decomposition with factor
C^{\prime}
.

(ii) All Mano decompositions of
M
are obtained that way.

Proof. -
(i) comes by a mechanical calculation.

(ii) Let
M=P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}
a Mano decomposition with factor
C^{\prime}
. Set

\Lambda:={P^{\prime}}^{-1}P=Q^{\prime}Q^{-1}
. Since
{P^{\prime}}^{-1}P
has poles only at

[x_{1},x_{2};q]
and
Q^{\prime}Q^{-1}
at
[x_{3},x_{4};q]
and since by (NR) these sets
do not meet,
\Lambda\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathcal{O}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})})
. From

(\sigma_{q}P)CP^{-1}=\dfrac{1}{x}R=(\sigma_{q}P^{\prime})C^{\prime}{P^{\prime}}^{-1}
we draw that

C^{\prime}=\Lambda[C]
.

\Box

Actually,
\Lambda
is a Laurent polynomial (with matrix coefficients). Indeed,
write
\Lambda=\sum x^{n}\Lambda_{n}
. Then relation
C^{\prime}=\Lambda[C]
implies
that
C^{\prime}\Lambda_{n}=q^{n}\Lambda_{n}C
. But equation with matricial unknown
X

C^{\prime}X-X(q^{n}C)=0
has non trivial solutions if and only if
{\text{Sp}}C^{\prime}
and

{\text{Sp}}(q^{n}C)
intersect, which is possible only for a finite number of values of
n
.
This statement can be made more precise using proposition
5.10

and its corollary.

5.2.3
Normal forms for
C

Proposition 5.10

(Normal forms for
C
)

The central factor
C
can be taken in one and only one of the following forms:

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&0\\
0&\xi\end{pmatrix}\quad

(trivial form)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}&0\\
0&\xi_{2}\end{pmatrix},\;\;\;\xi_{1}\neq\xi_{2}\quad

\displaystyle\text{(generic form)},

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\xi\\
0&\xi\end{pmatrix}\quad

\displaystyle\text{(logarithmic form)},

with
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
or
\xi
in the fundamental annulus
{\mathcal{C}_{q}}:|q|<|z|\leq 1
.

Moreover, the form is unique except that in generic form
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
can be permuted.

Proof. -
Among gauge transforms are conjugacies (by
{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})
) so, by standard reduction
theory,
C
can be taken diagonal or in the form

\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\xi\\
0&\xi\end{pmatrix}
. Then gauge transformation
by so called “shearing matrices”
{\text{Diag}}(x^{\mu},x^{\nu})
,
\mu,\nu\in{\mathbf{Z}}
, allows
one to bring
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
or
\xi
into
{\mathcal{C}_{q}}
. The possibility to permute

\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
comes from the equality:

\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{2}&0\\
0&\xi_{1}\end{pmatrix}=J\left[\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}&0\\
0&\xi_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right],\text{\penalty\ where\penalty\ }J:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}.

We now show that this is the only defect of uniqueness. So let
C,C^{\prime}
in one of
the quoted forms and let
\Lambda=(\lambda_{i,j})_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathcal{O}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})})

such that
\Lambda[C]=C^{\prime}
, or, equivalently,
\sigma_{q}\Lambda=C^{\prime}\Lambda C^{-1}
.

Case 1,
C
and
C^{\prime}
generic or trivial:
Write
C={\text{Diag}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})

and
C^{\prime}={\text{Diag}}(\xi^{\prime}_{1},\xi^{\prime}_{2})
, whence

\sigma_{q}\lambda_{i,j}=\dfrac{\xi^{\prime}_{i}}{\xi_{j}}\lambda_{i,j}
. By section

2.4
we know that this is possible with

\lambda_{i,j}\in{\mathcal{O}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})}\setminus\{0\}
only if
\dfrac{\xi^{\prime}_{i}}{\xi_{j}}\in q^{\mathbf{Z}}
;
since
\xi^{\prime}_{i},\xi_{j}\in{\mathcal{C}_{q}}
, this would mean
\xi^{\prime}_{i}=\xi_{j}
and

\lambda_{i,j}\in{\mathbf{C}}
. The end of the proof is standard linear algebra.

Case 2,
C
and
C^{\prime}
logarithmic:

Write
C=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\xi\\
0&\xi\end{pmatrix}

C^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi^{\prime}&\xi^{\prime}\\
0&\xi^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}
, whence:

\sigma_{q}\Lambda=\dfrac{\xi^{\prime}}{\xi}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1,1}+\lambda_{2,1}&\lambda_{1,2}+\lambda_{2,2}-(\lambda_{1,1}+\lambda_{2,1})\\
\lambda_{2,1}&\lambda_{2,2}-\lambda_{2,1}\end{pmatrix}.

If
\xi^{\prime}\neq\xi
, equation
\sigma_{q}\lambda_{2,1}=\dfrac{\xi^{\prime}}{\xi}\lambda_{2,1}

implies
\lambda_{2,1}=0
; then equation

\sigma_{q}\lambda_{1,1}=\dfrac{\xi^{\prime}}{\xi}\lambda_{1,1}
implies
\lambda_{1,1}=0
;
but then
\Lambda
is not invertible, contradiction.

So
\xi^{\prime}=\xi
and
\lambda_{2,1}\in{\mathbf{C}}
. But then we know from subsection

2.4.2
that
\sigma_{q}\lambda_{1,1}=\lambda_{1,1}+\lambda_{2,1}

is possible with
\lambda_{1,1}\in{\mathcal{O}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})}
only if
\lambda_{2,1}=0
. The end
of the proof along the same lines is easy.

Case 3, mixed case:
similar calculations left to the reader show
this case to be impossible because there is no
q
-logarithm in
{\mathcal{O}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})}
.

\Box

From the above proof, one can also draw:

Corollary 5.11

If
C
is in normal form, then

\Lambda[C]=C\Longleftrightarrow\left(\Lambda\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }[\Lambda,C]=0\right)
.

\Box

5.3
Necessary conditions for the equality
M=PQ

As a preliminary observation, note that if
M=PQ
is a Mano decomposition with
factor
C
in normal form, then we can still replace
P
by any
P^{\prime}=P\Lambda^{-1}

where
\Lambda\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})
commutes with
C
. The apparently more general case

\Lambda\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathcal{O}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})})
,
\Lambda[C]=C
boils down to this one by
corollary
5.11
.

5.3.1
Possible forms of the factor
P

We can (and will) search the factor
C
in one of the forms shown in proposition

5.10
. We also know from the property of
\det C
stated in

5.2
that, according to the case,

\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\equiv(\rho_{1}\rho_{2})/(x_{1}x_{2})
or

\xi^{2}\equiv(\rho_{1}\rho_{2})/(x_{1}x_{2})
.

Trivial form.

In the case
C=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&0\\
0&\xi\end{pmatrix}
, we must have:

P=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{1}}x\right)&\alpha_{1,2}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{1}}x\right)\\
\alpha_{2,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{2}}x\right)&\alpha_{2,2}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{2}}x\right)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{1}}x\right)&0\\
0&{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{2}}x\right)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1,1}&\alpha_{1,2}\\
\alpha_{2,1}&\alpha_{2,2}\end{pmatrix},

for some
\alpha_{i,j}\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
i,j=1,2
. Thus

\det P=0\Leftrightarrow\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}-\alpha_{1,2}\alpha_{2,1}=0
.
Actually, the constant right factor
(\alpha_{i,j})
can be taken rid of by the
observation at the beginning of this section,
i.e.
we can take:

P=\begin{pmatrix}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{1}}x\right)&0\\
0&{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{2}}x\right)\end{pmatrix}.

Generic form.

In the case
C=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}&0\\
0&\xi_{2}\end{pmatrix}
,

\xi_{1}\not\equiv\xi_{2}
, we must have:

P=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right)&\alpha_{1,2}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{1}}x\right)\\
\alpha_{2,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)&\alpha_{2,2}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)\end{pmatrix},

for some
\alpha_{i,j}\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
i,j=1,2
. Then:

\det P=\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)-\alpha_{1,2}\alpha_{2,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{1}}x\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right),

so that:

\det P=0\Longleftrightarrow\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}=\alpha_{1,2}\alpha_{2,1}=0.

Indeed, since
\xi_{1}\not\equiv\xi_{2}
, because of (NR) the functions

{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)

and

{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{1}}x\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)

have no common zero.

Logarithmic form.

In the case
C=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\xi\\
0&\xi\end{pmatrix}
,
the coefficients
p_{i,j}
of
P
satisfy

\begin{cases}\sigma_{q}p_{i,1}=\frac{\rho_{i}}{\xi}p_{i,1},\\
\sigma_{q}p_{i,2}=\frac{\rho_{i}}{\xi}(p_{i,2}-p_{i,1}),\end{cases}

i=1,2
.
According to lemma
2.1
in subsection
2.4.2
,
setting
\phi_{i}(x):={\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{i}}x\right)
and

\psi_{i}(x):=x\phi_{i}^{\prime}(x)=\frac{\xi}{\rho_{i}}x{\theta_{q}^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{i}}x\right)
,
we must have:

P=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1,1}\phi_{1}&\alpha_{1,1}\psi_{1}+\alpha_{1,2}\phi_{1}\\
\alpha_{2,1}\phi_{2}&\alpha_{2,1}\psi_{2}+\alpha_{2,2}\phi_{2}\end{pmatrix}

for some
\alpha_{i,j}\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
i,j=1,2
, so that

\displaystyle\det P

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}-\alpha_{2,1}\alpha_{1,2})\phi_{1}\phi_{2}+\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,1}x(\phi_{1}\phi_{2}^{\prime}-\phi_{2}\phi_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=x\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\times\text{\penalty\ logarithmic derivative of\penalty\ }\left(x^{\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}-\alpha_{2,1}\alpha_{1,2}}\left(\frac{\phi_{2}}{\phi_{1}}\right)^{\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,1}}\right),

whence

\det P=0\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,1}=0\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\\
\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}-\alpha_{2,1}\alpha_{1,2}=0\end{cases}\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}(\alpha_{1,1}=\alpha_{2,1}=0)\text{\penalty\ or\penalty\ }\\
(\alpha_{1,1}=\alpha_{1,2}=0)\text{\penalty\ or\penalty\ }\\
(\alpha_{2,1}=\alpha_{2,2}=0).\end{cases}

5.3.2
Further necessary conditions

From lemma
5.8
, we know that,
P
being given,

(p_{1}q_{2}:p_{2}q_{1})\in{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
is well defined. We identify
this point of the projective line with

\dfrac{p_{1}q_{2}}{p_{2}q_{1}}\in{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{C}}\cup\{\infty\}
, considered as
the target space of elliptic functions, in particular of the function

\Phi
defined in
5.1.3
.

In what follows, we write
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
the eigenvalues of
C
, with
maybe (in the trivial or in the logarithmic case)
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}
.
Their images
\overline{\xi_{1}},\overline{\xi_{2}}\in{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
are the

exponents
of
C
.

Lemma 5.12

In all cases above, the exponents
\overline{\xi_{1}},\overline{\xi_{2}}

constitute the fiber
\Phi^{-1}\left(\dfrac{p_{1}q_{2}}{p_{2}q_{1}}\right)
,

i.e.
:

\Phi^{-1}\left(\dfrac{p_{1}q_{2}}{p_{2}q_{1}}\right)=\left\{\overline{\xi_{1}},\overline{\xi_{2}}\right\}.

Proof. -
Since
\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\equiv(\rho_{1}\rho_{2})/(x_{1}x_{2})
, it will be
enough to prove that:

\Phi(\xi_{1})=\dfrac{p_{1}q_{2}}{p_{2}q_{1}}\cdot

In all cases above, the first column of
P(x)
is

A(x):=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right)\\
\alpha_{2,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)\end{pmatrix}
.

If
A(x_{1})
and
A(x_{2})
are both non zero, we can take them as column
vectors
\begin{pmatrix}p_{i}\\
q_{i}\end{pmatrix}
. An immediate calculation
then gives
\dfrac{p_{1}q_{2}}{p_{2}q_{1}}=\Phi(\xi_{1})
as wanted.

Assume that for instance
A(x_{1})=0
(the case
A(x_{2})=0
being entirely
similar). We have

\alpha_{1,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right)=\alpha_{2,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)=0
.
We cannot have
\alpha_{1,1}=\alpha_{2,1}=0
because then
\det P
would
vanish identically; neither can we have

{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right)={\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)=0
, because of condition (NR).
Therefore, we have two cases to consider:

1.

\alpha_{1,1}\neq 0
,
\alpha_{2,1}=0
and

{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right)=0
. Then
\Phi(\xi_{1})=0
.
Also
{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)\neq 0
, so
A(x_{2})\neq 0
,
so we can take it as
\begin{pmatrix}p_{2}\\
q_{2}\end{pmatrix}
, so

q_{2}=\alpha_{2,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)=0
, so

\dfrac{p_{1}q_{2}}{p_{2}q_{1}}=0=\Phi(\xi_{1})
as wanted.

2.

\alpha_{1,1}=0
,
\alpha_{2,1}\neq 0
and

{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)=0
. A similar calculation
yields
\Phi(\xi_{1})=\dfrac{p_{1}q_{2}}{p_{2}q_{1}}=\infty
.

\Box

5.4
Existence of Mano decomposition

Let
R,S,{\underline{x}}
the local data described in subsection

5.1
, subject to the assumptions (FR), (NR)
and (NS) stated there.

Let
\Phi(\xi):=\dfrac{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{x_{1}}{\rho_{1}}\xi\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{x_{2}}{\rho_{2}}\xi\right)}{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{x_{1}}{\rho_{2}}\xi\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{x_{2}}{\rho_{1}}\xi\right)}

the elliptic function defined and studied in subsection

5.1.3
.

For every matrix
M\in F_{R,S,{\underline{x}}}
, recall
\Pi(M):=(f_{1}g_{2}:f_{2}g_{1})\in{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}

the projective invariant defined and studied in subsection

5.1.1
.

Theorem 5.13

(Existence of Mano decomposition)

Every matrix
M\in F_{R,S,{\underline{x}}}
admits a Mano decomposition
M=PQ
with (some)
factor
C
. More precisely:

1.

If
\Pi(M)
is not a critical value of
\Phi
, the decomposition is
generic (
i.e.
the factor
C
is in generic form).

2.

If
\Pi(M)
is a critical value of
\Phi
and
\Pi(M)\neq 0,\infty
,
the decomposition is logarithmic.

3.

If
\Pi(M)
is a critical value of
\Phi
and
\Pi(M)=0
or
\infty
,
the decomposition is logarithmic, except for the respective degenerate cases:

f_{1}=g_{2}=0
and
f_{2}=g_{1}=0
; in these degenerate cases, the decomposition
is trivial.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof. We shall keep the notations

\begin{pmatrix}f_{i}\\
g_{i}\end{pmatrix}
,
i=1,2
, for a non zero column
of
M(x_{i})
(
5.1.1
) and similarly

\begin{pmatrix}p_{i}\\
q_{i}\end{pmatrix}
,
i=1,2
, for a non zero column
of the left factor
P(x_{i})
(
5.2.1
).

We write the fiber of
\Phi
at
\Pi(M)\in{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
as:

\Phi^{-1}\left(\Pi(M)\right)=\Phi^{-1}\left(\dfrac{f_{1}g_{2}}{f_{2}g_{1}}\right)=\left\{\overline{\xi_{1}},\overline{\xi_{2}}\right\},

where
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}_{q}}
(subsection
5.1.3
).
Thus we have:

(5.13.1)

\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\equiv\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}.

5.4.1
Preliminary reductions

Proposition 5.14

In order to prove theorem
5.13
, it is enough to find a factor
C

and
P\in{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathcal{O}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})})
such that:

1.

\sigma_{q}P=RP(Cx)^{-1}
,

2.

\det P\neq 0
and
\det P
vanishes at
x_{1}
,

3.

P^{-1}M
is well defined (
i.e.
has no pole) at
x_{1},x_{2}
.

Proof. -
By
2.4
,
\det P
vanishes at
[x_{1},x_{2};q]

and
Q:=P^{-1}M
vanishes at
[x_{3},x_{4};q]
under the same conditions
as usual and immediate calculation shows that we got a Mano decomposition.

\Box

Now, from Cramer’s rule
P^{-1}=(\det P)^{-1}\tilde{P}
, we see that the
last condition can be replaced by:
(\tilde{P}M)(x_{i})=0
,
i=1,2
. This
in turn is equivalent to:
\begin{pmatrix}f_{i}\\
g_{i}\end{pmatrix}
is a
linear combination of the columns of
P(x_{i})
for
i=1,2
,
i.e.
that it
is proportional to the selected non zero columns.

Corollary 5.15

Condition 3 above can be replaced by:

3’.

\begin{pmatrix}f_{i}\\
g_{i}\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathbf{C}}\begin{pmatrix}p_{i}\\
q_{i}\end{pmatrix},i=1,2
.

Using lemmas
5.4
and
5.8
,
we complete the above as follows:

Proposition 5.16

Let
P
a left factor of
M
in a Mano decomposition and keep previous
notations
\begin{pmatrix}f_{i}\\
g_{i}\end{pmatrix}
and

\begin{pmatrix}p_{i}\\
q_{i}\end{pmatrix}
,
i=1,2
. Then one cannot have

p_{1}q_{2}=p_{2}q_{1}=0
, the ratio
(p_{1}q_{2}:p_{2}q_{1})\in{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}

is well defined from
P
and
(p_{1}q_{2}:p_{2}q_{1})=(f_{1}g_{2}:f_{2}g_{1})
.

5.4.2
Proof of existence, case I:
f_{1}f_{2}g_{1}g_{2}\neq 0

From the proposition above,
p_{1}p_{2}q_{1}q_{2}\neq 0
. We shall use lemma

5.4
.

Subcase Ia:
\xi_{1}\neq\xi_{2}
.

For some
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in{\mathbf{C}}
to be determined (not both zero), we set:

P_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right)&\alpha_{1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{1}}x\right)\\
{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)&\alpha_{2}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }C:=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}&0\\
0&\xi_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Then, writing

T_{1}(x):={\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)

and

T_{2}(x):={\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{1}}x\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)
,
we have:

\det P_{0}=\alpha_{2}T_{1}-\alpha_{1}T_{2},

so taking
\alpha_{1}:=T_{2}(x_{1})
and
\alpha_{2}:=T_{1}(x_{1})
(which, by (NR),
cannot both be
0
), we see that
P_{0}
satisfies the first two conditions of
proposition
5.14
. Writing

\begin{pmatrix}p_{i}\\
q_{i}\end{pmatrix}
the first column of
P_{0}(x_{i})
,

i=1,2
, we fall, by choice of
\xi_{1}
, under the assumptions of lemma

5.4
. We set

\Lambda:=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&\mu\end{pmatrix}
with

\lambda,\mu
as provided by lemma
5.4

and then
P:=\Lambda P_{0}
. Using the fact that
\Lambda
and
C

commute with each other, we easily conclude that
P
satisfies all three
conditions of proposition
5.14
, so theorem

5.13
is proved in this case.

Subcase Ib:
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=:\xi
.

From the definition of
\Phi
, we see that

{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{x_{j}}{\rho_{i}}\xi\right)\neq 0
for
i,j=1,2
.
Writing
\phi_{i}(x):={\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi x}{\rho_{i}}\right)
,
i=1,2
,
it follows that
(\phi_{1}\phi_{2})(x_{1})\neq 0
.

We take
C:=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\xi\\
0&\xi\end{pmatrix}

and set
P_{0}:=\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{1}&x\phi^{\prime}_{1}+\alpha_{1}\phi_{1}\\
\phi_{2}&x\phi^{\prime}_{2}+\alpha_{2}\phi_{2}\end{pmatrix}
,
so that
\sigma_{q}P_{0}=RP(Cx)^{-1}
. Also

\det P_{0}=(\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{1})\phi_{1}\phi_{2}+x(\phi_{1}\phi^{\prime}_{2}-\phi^{\prime}_{1}\phi_{2})

is equal to
x\phi_{1}\phi_{2}
times the logarithmic derivative of

x^{\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{1}}\frac{\phi_{2}}{\phi_{1}}
and certainly does not vanish identically.

Since
(\phi_{1}\phi_{2})(x_{1})\neq 0
, a proper choice of
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}

yields
\det P_{0}(x_{1})=0
and the argument can be completed exactly as in
subcase Ia to obtain a left factor
P:=\Lambda P_{0}
.

5.4.3
Proof of existence, case II:
f_{1}f_{2}g_{1}g_{2}=0

Here we have
(f_{1}g_{2})(f_{2}g_{1})=0
while, by lemma
5.3
,

f_{1}g_{2}
and
f_{2}g_{1}
cannot both be zero. If
f_{1}g_{2}=0\neq f_{2}g_{1}
, we
must have
\Phi(\xi_{1})=\Phi(\xi_{2})=0
; if
f_{1}g_{2}\neq 0=f_{2}g_{1}
, we must
have
\Phi(\xi_{1})=\Phi(\xi_{2})=\infty
. We consider the former case only, the
latter being entirely similar.
Thus we have
\Phi^{-1}(0)=\left\{\overline{\xi_{1}},\overline{\xi_{2}}\right\}
.

Subcase IIa:
\xi_{1}\neq\xi_{2}
.

If
f_{1}=0
, we take, for some
\alpha\in{\mathbf{C}}
to be determined:

P=\begin{pmatrix}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right)&0\\
\alpha{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)&{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)\end{pmatrix}.

Then
\det P={\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)

does not vanish identically; moreover:

P(x_{1})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\alpha\ast&\ast\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\quad P(x_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}\ast&0\\
\alpha\ast&0\end{pmatrix},

where each
\ast
stands for some non zero complex number. Both determinants
vanish, as required. We can take as
\begin{pmatrix}p_{1}\\
q_{1}\end{pmatrix}

the right column of
P(x_{1})
, which is indeed non zero and proportional to

\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}\\
g_{1}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\ast\end{pmatrix}
.
Likewise, we can take as

\begin{pmatrix}p_{2}\\
q_{2}\end{pmatrix}
the left column of
P(x_{2})
, which
is indeed non zero and can be made proportional to

\begin{pmatrix}f_{2}\\
g_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\ast\\
g_{2}\end{pmatrix}
by an appropriate choice of

\alpha
. This terminates the proof in this case.

If
g_{2}=0
, we take, for some
\alpha\in{\mathbf{C}}
to be determined:

P=\begin{pmatrix}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right)&\alpha{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{1}}x\right)\\
0&{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)\end{pmatrix}.

We leave to the reader to complete the argument in this case.

Subcase IIb:
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=:\xi
.

Then
\xi\equiv-\dfrac{\rho_{1}}{\sigma_{1}}\equiv-\dfrac{\rho_{2}}{\sigma_{2}}

and
0
is a critical value
44
44

44

In the case
f_{2}g_{1}=0
and
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}
,
we would have

\xi\equiv-\dfrac{\rho_{1}}{\sigma_{2}}\equiv-\dfrac{\rho_{2}}{\sigma_{1}}
and
the critical value would be
\infty
.
of
\Phi
. If
f_{1}=0
, one has

M(x_{1})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\ast&\ast\end{pmatrix}
and we want
p_{1}=0
.
If
g_{2}=0
, one has

M(x_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}\ast&\ast\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
and we want
q_{2}=0
.

IIb (i):
f_{1}=g_{2}=0
.

We take
C:={\text{Diag}}(\xi,\xi)
and

P:={\text{Diag}}\left({\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{1}}x\right),{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{2}}x\right)\right)
.
The right column of
P(x_{1})
is
\begin{pmatrix}p_{1}\\
q_{1}\end{pmatrix}
and
the left column of
P(x_{2})
is
\begin{pmatrix}p_{2}\\
q_{2}\end{pmatrix}
.

IIb (ii):
f_{1}=0
,
g_{2}\neq 0
.

We take
C:=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\xi\\
0&\xi\end{pmatrix}
. We take

P:=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1,1}\phi_{1}&\alpha_{1,1}\psi_{1}+\alpha_{1,2}\phi_{1}\\
\alpha_{2,1}\phi_{2}&\alpha_{2,1}\psi_{2}+\alpha_{2,2}\phi_{2}\end{pmatrix}

for some
\alpha_{i,j}\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
i,j=1,2
with the usual notations for
\phi_{i}

(see subsection
5.3.1
). We have here

\phi_{1}(x_{1})=\phi_{2}(x_{2})=0
and, as a consequence,

\phi_{1}^{\prime}(x_{1}),\phi_{2}^{\prime}(x_{2}),\phi_{2}(x_{1}),\phi_{1}(x_{2})\neq 0
.
Then
P(x_{1})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\ast&?\end{pmatrix}
, its left column
is
\begin{pmatrix}p_{1}\\
q_{1}\end{pmatrix}
, indeed colinear with

\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}\\
g_{1}\end{pmatrix}
.

Also, setting
\alpha_{1,1}:=0
and
\alpha_{2,1}:=1
, one has

P(x_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}0&\alpha_{1,2}\phi_{1}(x_{2})\\
0&\ast\end{pmatrix}
, its
right column can be chosen as
\begin{pmatrix}p_{2}\\
q_{2}\end{pmatrix}
and made
colinear to
\begin{pmatrix}f_{2}\\
g_{2}\end{pmatrix}
by an appropriate choice
of
\alpha_{1,2}
.

IIb (iii):
f_{1}\neq 0
,
g_{2}=0
.
We leave to the reader to find
the argument in this case (symmetric of the previous one).

This ends the proof of the theorem.

6
The Jimbo-Sakai family (II)

We first recall our general assumptions on the Jimbo-Sakai family, as described
in section
4.1
and completed in section

5.1
. The local data are:

R:={\text{Diag}}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}),\quad S:={\text{Diag}}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\quad\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\quad{\underline{x}}:=\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\}.

We assume
Fuchs relation
(FR),
strong non resonancy
(NR) and add

non splitting
(NS) with respect to the selected pair

{\underline{x}}^{\prime}:=\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
. We also write
{\underline{x}}^{\prime\prime}:=\{x_{3},x_{4}\}
.

Our goal here is to give a geometric description of the
monodromy data
space

{\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{F}}_{R,s,{\underline{x}}}
underlying the Jimbo-Sakai approach to the study of
the discrete Painlevé equation
q
-PVI. We gave such a (crude) description
in section
4
. In this section, we obtain a more
general and more precise description, using for that the Mano decomposition
studied in section
5
.

In hope that the reader doesn’t get lost in the maze of computations of cases
and subcases, here is a brief summary of the process. In short,
{\mathcal{F}}
will be
considered as fibered over the base consisting of all possible factors
C
in
the Mano decomposition:

1.

The space
X
of possible matrices
C
is that of all those
C\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})

such that
\det C
is compatible with the prescribed local data:

\det C\equiv\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}\equiv\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}x_{3}x_{4}
.

2.

Since
{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
, the fiber over
C
, is really determined by the class of
C
under
gauge equivalence,
the true base space
B
of our fibration is a quotient of
X
. We describe it
with the help of normal forms for
C
; generically they are diagonal and unique
up to permutation of the diagonal terms, so we should take in account an involution.

3.

The space
{\mathcal{F}}
is the quotient of
F:=F_{R,s,{\underline{x}}}
by the equivalence relation

\sim
, which was defined in
3.1.2
.
Let
F_{C}
the subspace of
F
made up of those matrices admitting Mano
decomposition with factor
C
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{C}:=\frac{F_{C}}{\sim}
. We parameterize
each
F_{C}
by some space of complex matrices
45
45

45

As in all the paper, this
is possible because the spaces of solutions of “elementary”
q
-difference
equations come equipped with explicit finite bases.
and then each
{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
can
be described with the help of some (multi-)linear algebra.

4.

Of course the description of
{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
is not the same in the
generic
,

trivial
46
46

46

Actually the trivial case will be excluded
de facto
,
see herebelow
6.1.1
.
and
logarithmic
cases.
But moreover in the generic case there are some
special
values for which
the fiber is not the same as the
general
fiber (for instance fibers related
to those we encountered in
5.1.2
).

5.

Last we rebuild
{\mathcal{F}}
as he union of the fibers
{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
.

6.1
An assumption on the local data and a preliminary consequence

In
6.3.3
, we shall be led to introduce two subsets of
the fundamental annulus
{\mathcal{C}_{q}}
, related to the special fibers mentioned hereabove.
For
x\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
, recall (from the
q
-notations in
2.2
)
that we write
R(x)
the unique representative of
x
in
{\mathcal{C}_{q}}
(
i.e.

R(x)\in{\mathcal{C}_{q}}

and
R(x)\equiv x
). Let:

\displaystyle\Xi^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\{R(-\rho_{1}/x_{1}),R(-\rho_{1}/x_{2}),R(-\rho_{2}/x_{1}),R(-\rho_{2}/x_{2})\},

\displaystyle\Xi^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle:=\{R(-\sigma_{1}x_{3}),R(-\sigma_{1}x_{4}),R(-\sigma_{2}x_{3}),R(-\sigma_{2}x_{4})\}.

Then, in order to simplify the exposition, we shall assume
47
47

47

Our analysis
can easily be extended to other cases, yielding similar though slightly different
geometries.
(see equation (
6.4.1
)) that:

\text{Assumption\penalty\ }\mathbf{Hyp}_{8}:\quad\Xi:=\Xi^{\prime}\cup\Xi^{\prime\prime}\text{\penalty\ has eight (pairwise distinct) elements.}

6.1.1
Our assumption excludes the trivial case

We draw at once a consequence of this assumption:
the trivial case for
C

(see the third situation described in theorem
5.13
, page

5.13
) cannot occur.

Indeed, by
5.3.1
, in the case

C=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&0\\
0&\xi\end{pmatrix}
, we must have:

P=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{1}}x\right)&\alpha_{1,2}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{1}}x\right)\\
\alpha_{2,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{2}}x\right)&\alpha_{2,2}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{2}}x\right)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{1}}x\right)&0\\
0&{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{2}}x\right)\end{pmatrix}A,\text{\penalty\ where\penalty\ }A:=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1,1}&\alpha_{1,2}\\
\alpha_{2,1}&\alpha_{2,2}\end{pmatrix},

In the same way:

Q=\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{1,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\xi}x\right)&\beta_{1,2}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\xi}x\right)\\
\beta_{2,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\xi}x\right)&\beta_{2,2}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\xi}x\right)\end{pmatrix}=B\begin{pmatrix}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\xi}x\right)&0\\
0&{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\xi}x\right)\end{pmatrix},\text{\penalty\ where\penalty\ }B:=\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{1,1}&\beta_{1,2}\\
\beta_{2,1}&\beta_{2,2}\end{pmatrix},

Usual conditions on
P,Q
imply first that
\det A,\det B\neq 0
; and then,
since
\det P(x_{1})=\det Q(x_{3})=0
, that:

\big(\xi\equiv-\rho_{1}/x_{1}\text{\penalty\ or\penalty\ }\xi\equiv-\rho_{2}/x_{1}\big)\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\big(\xi\equiv-\sigma_{1}x_{3}\text{\penalty\ or\penalty\ }\xi\equiv-\sigma_{2}x_{3}\big).

This would imply
\xi\in\Xi^{\prime}\cap\Xi^{\prime\prime}
, which, by assumption, is impossible.

6.1.2
Under our assumption,
0
and
\infty
cannot be critical values
of
\Phi

This means that case 3 in theorem
5.13
cannot occur at all,
so this statement actually subsumes the statement hereabove about the
impossibility of the trivial case.

The argument is the following: if for instance
0
is a critical value, then
from the definition of
\Phi
(recalled at the beginning of subsection

5.4
, just before theorem
5.13
),

-\rho_{1}/x_{1}\equiv-\rho_{2}/x_{2}
. Similarly, if
\infty
is a critical value,
then
-\rho_{1}/x_{2}\equiv-\rho_{2}/x_{1}
. Both congruences are excluded by the
fact that all elements of
\Xi^{\prime}
are distinct.

6.2
Fibration of
{\mathcal{F}}

Recall that
{\mathcal{F}}=F/\sim
where
F:=F_{R,s,{\underline{x}}}
and the equivalence relation

\sim
is defined by
M\sim M^{(\Gamma,\Delta)}:=\Gamma M\Delta^{-1}
, where

\Gamma
and
\Delta
are diagonal matrices. It is induced by the group action
of the product of the group of
2\times 2
invertible diagonal matrices by
itself (actually, by the free action of the
3
-dimensional torus

\dfrac{{\text{D}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})}\times{\text{D}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})}}{{\mathbf{C}}^{*}(I_{2},I_{2})}
, see
4.2
).

6.2.1
Partition of
F

Let

X:=\left\{C\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})\mid\det C\equiv\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}\right\},

the set of possible factors
C
for Mano decompositions. For all
C\in X
,
set:

\displaystyle F_{C}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\left\{P\in{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathcal{O}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})})\Bigm|\begin{cases}\sigma_{q}P=RP(Cx)^{-1},\\
\det P\neq 0,\\
\det P\text{\penalty\ vanishes on\penalty\ }{\underline{x}}^{\prime}\end{cases}\right\}

\displaystyle F_{C}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle:=\left\{Q\in{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathcal{O}({\mathbf{C}}^{*})})\Bigm|\begin{cases}\sigma_{q}Q=CQ(Sx)^{-1},\\
\det Q\neq 0,\\
\det Q\text{\penalty\ vanishes on\penalty\ }{\underline{x}}^{\prime\prime}\end{cases}\right\}

Thus, as usual, all zeroes of
\det P
and
\det Q
are simple and located
on
[{\underline{x}}^{\prime};q]
and
[{\underline{x}}^{\prime\prime};q]
respectively.

For every
C\in X
, we have a well defined product map:

\displaystyle F_{C}^{\prime}\times F_{C}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\rightarrow F,

\displaystyle(P,Q)

\displaystyle\mapsto PQ.

We call
F_{C}
its image: it is the set of those
M\in F
that admit
a Mano decomposition with factor
C
. By theorem
5.13
:

F=\bigcup_{C\in X}F_{C}.

Also, by proposition
5.9
,
F_{C_{1}}=F_{C_{2}}
if, and only
if,
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are gauge equivalent; otherwise
F_{C_{1}}
and
F_{C_{2}}

are disjoint. So, writing
\sim
the restriction of the gauge equivalence
relation to
X
, with a slight abuse of notation (confusing
C
with its class
in
X/\sim
) we have a partition:

F=\bigsqcup_{C\in\frac{X}{\sim}}F_{C}.

6.2.2
Partition of
{\mathcal{F}}

In order to apply the partition of
F
to the quotient
{\mathcal{F}}=F/\sim
, we need
to complete proposition
5.9
(gauge freedom for
C
).

Proposition 6.1

Let
C\in N
and let
(P_{1},Q_{1}),(P_{2},Q_{2})\in F_{C}^{\prime}\times F_{C}^{\prime\prime}
. Then:

P_{1}Q_{1}\sim P_{2}Q_{2}\Longleftrightarrow(P_{2},Q_{2})=(\Gamma P_{1}\Lambda^{-1},\Lambda Q_{1}\Delta^{-1})\text{\penalty\ for some\penalty\ }\Gamma,\Delta\text{\penalty\ diagonal and\penalty\ }\Lambda\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})\text{\penalty\ such that\penalty\ }\Lambda C=C\Lambda.

Proof. -
We have
P_{2}Q_{2}=\Gamma P_{1}Q_{1}\Delta^{-1}
for some
\Gamma,\Delta

diagonal and
(\Gamma P_{1})(Q_{1}\Delta^{-1})
is another Mano decomposition
with factor
C
for
P_{2}Q_{2}
, so proposition
5.9

yields some
\Lambda
such that
\Lambda[C]=C
. Since
C
is normalized,

\Lambda\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})
and
\Lambda C=C\Lambda
.

\Box

As a consequence, we define on each
F_{C}^{\prime}\times F_{C}^{\prime\prime}
,
C\in N
, an
equivalence relation by:

(P_{1},Q_{1})\sim(P_{2},Q_{2})\underset{def}{\Longleftrightarrow}(P_{2},Q_{2})=(\Gamma P_{1}\Lambda^{-1},\Lambda Q_{1}\Delta^{-1})\text{\penalty\ for some\penalty\ }\Gamma,\Delta\text{\penalty\ diagonal and\penalty\ }\Lambda\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})\text{\penalty\ such that\penalty\ }\Lambda C=C\Lambda.

We then deduce from the discussion in
6.2.1
that:

{\mathcal{F}}=\bigsqcup_{C\in\frac{X}{\sim}}{\mathcal{F}}_{C},

where we have a well defined bijection:

\dfrac{F_{C}^{\prime}\times F_{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\sim}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{F}}_{C}.

6.2.3
The base space of the fibration

A priori
, we should take as base space of our fibration the quotient

\dfrac{X}{\sim}
of the set
X\subset{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})
by the gauge equivalence
relation. However, under the assumption formulated in subsection

6.1
we saw in
6.1.1

that the
trivial case
is impossible and reduced scalar matrices may be
excluded.

For a more precise description, we shall use the normal forms for
C
found
in
5.2.3
, proposition
5.10
;
we define the following subsets of
X
:

\displaystyle N_{g}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}&0\\
0&\xi_{2}\end{pmatrix}\Bigm|\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}_{q}}\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\xi_{1}\neq\xi_{2}\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\equiv\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}\right\},

\displaystyle N_{t}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\xi&0\\
0&\xi\end{pmatrix}\Bigm|\xi\in{\mathcal{C}_{q}}\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\xi^{2}\equiv\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}\right\},

\displaystyle N_{u}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\xi\\
0&\xi\end{pmatrix}\Bigm|\xi\in{\mathcal{C}_{q}}\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\xi^{2}\equiv\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}\right\},

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle:=N_{g}\sqcup N_{t}\sqcup N_{u},

\displaystyle N^{*}

\displaystyle:=N_{g}\sqcup N_{u}.

Every element of
X
is equivalent to a element of
N
and the only possible
non trivial equivalences within
N
are relations within
N_{g}
of the form

{\text{Diag}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\sim{\text{Diag}}(\xi_{2},\xi_{1})
. Factors
C\in N_{g}
, resp.

C\in N_{t}
, resp.
C\in N_{u}
correspond to what we called the generic, resp.
the trivial, resp. the logarithmic case. Since any element in the group
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}

has exactly
4
square roots (recall that we use the multiplicative notation),

{\text{card}}\ N_{t}={\text{card}}\ N_{u}=4
.

Our true base space (
N_{t}
being excluded by
6.1.1
)
is therefore the quotient:

B:=\dfrac{N^{*}}{\sim},

where
\sim
is the relation induced by the involution trivial on
N_{t}
and
defined on
N_{g}
as
{\text{Diag}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\mapsto{\text{Diag}}(\xi_{2},\xi_{1})
. Sending

{\text{Diag}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
to
\xi_{1}
and

\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\xi\\
0&\xi\end{pmatrix}
to
\xi
defines a bijection
of
N^{*}
to
{\mathcal{C}_{q}}
, hence to
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
. The corresponding involution on
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
is the map:

\alpha\mapsto\dfrac{a}{\alpha},

where
a
is the class of
\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}
. We already met
this involution in
5.1.4
.

Proposition 6.2

As a quotient holomorphic curve, the base space
B
is isomorphic to the projective
line
{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
. The quotient map is realized by
\Phi
,
i.e.
it is the mapping:

N^{*}\rightarrow{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})},\quad\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\ast\\
\ast&\ast\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\Phi(\xi).

\Box

Let
M\in F
, let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
the eigenvalues of the factor
C
in the Mano
decomposition of
M
and
P
the left factor. The projective invariant
\Pi(M)

was defined after lemma
5.3
.

In the discussion at the beginning of
5.4
, we used lemma

5.12
, according to which:

\Phi^{-1}\left(\dfrac{p_{1}q_{2}}{p_{2}q_{1}}\right)=\left\{\overline{\xi_{1}},\overline{\xi_{2}}\right\},

where the
p_{i},q_{i}
are related to
P
as explained there. Then, in proposition

5.16
, we found that:

(p_{1}q_{2}:p_{2}q_{1})=(f_{1}g_{2}:f_{2}g_{1}),

where
(f_{1}g_{2}:f_{2}g_{1})=\Pi(M)
. Combining these facts, we find that:

\Pi(M)=\Phi(\xi_{1})=\Phi(\xi_{2}).

Using proposition
6.2
above, we can now recognize the true role
of the projective invariant
\Pi
:

Theorem 6.3

The fibration obtained from
F\rightarrow X
when going to the quotient is
(up to natural identifications):

{\mathcal{F}}\overset{\Pi}{\longrightarrow}{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}.

Proof. -
Recall from
6.2.1
the partition

F=\bigsqcup\limits_{C\in\frac{X}{\sim}}F_{C}
, which allows us to define a map

F\rightarrow\dfrac{X}{\sim}\cdot
The identification of
\dfrac{X}{\sim}

with
{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
is provided by the map
\Phi
. We get a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X/\sim\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{F}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}

The lower horizontal line is thus identified with the fibration.

\Box

6.3
Description of
{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
in the generic case

As can be guessed, we have to distinguish the generic and logarithmic cases for
C

(the trivial case has been excluded, see
6.1
).

Let
C={\text{Diag}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
, where
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}_{q}}
,
\xi_{1}\neq\xi_{2}

and
\det C=\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\equiv\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}
.
The matrices
\Lambda\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})
commuting with
C
are the diagonal
matrices.

6.3.1
Spaces of matrices

The elements of
F_{C}^{\prime}
are the matrices:

P=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right)&\alpha_{1,2}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{1}}x\right)\\
\alpha_{2,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)&\alpha_{2,2}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)\end{pmatrix}\text{\penalty\ for some\penalty\ }\alpha_{i,j}\in{\mathbf{C}},i,j=1,2,\text{\penalty\ such that\penalty\ }\begin{cases}(\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2},\alpha_{1,2}\alpha_{2,1})\neq(0,0),\\
\det P(x_{1})=\det P(x_{2})=0.\end{cases}

Condition
\det P(x_{1})=\det P(x_{2})=0
leads us to introduce the elliptic
function:

\Phi_{C}^{\prime}(x):=\dfrac{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)}{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{1}}x\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)},

which is of degree
2
since there is no cancellation of zeroes (this follows
from (NR) and the fact that we are in the generic case); also
\Phi_{C}^{\prime}
is such
that
\Phi_{C}^{\prime}(x_{1})=\Phi_{C}^{\prime}(x_{2})
(because of the condition on
\det C
). Then:

\left(\det P(x_{1})=\det P(x_{2})=0\right)\Longleftrightarrow\dfrac{\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}}{\alpha_{1,2}\alpha_{2,1}}=\dfrac{1}{\Phi_{C}^{\prime}(x_{i})},i=1,2.

Said equality is understood to hold in
{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}={\mathbf{C}}\cup\{\infty\}
. We are thus
led to set:

s:=\dfrac{1}{\Phi_{C}^{\prime}(x_{1})}=\dfrac{1}{\Phi_{C}^{\prime}(x_{2})}\in{\mathbf{C}}\cup\{\infty\}

and to define the following spaces of matrices; first:

{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})^{*}:=\{A:=(\alpha_{i,j})\in{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})\mid(\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2},\alpha_{1,2}\alpha_{2,1})\neq(0,0)\},

which we endow with a mapping:

\displaystyle\alpha:{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})^{*}

\displaystyle\rightarrow{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}={\mathbf{C}}\cup\{\infty\},

\displaystyle A:=(\alpha_{i,j})

\displaystyle\mapsto\dfrac{\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}}{\alpha_{1,2}\alpha_{2,1}}\cdot

Second:

{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}:=\{A:=(\alpha_{i,j})\in{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})^{*}\mid\alpha(A)=s\}.

We then have a bijection:

\displaystyle{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\rightarrow F_{C}^{\prime},

\displaystyle A:=(\alpha_{i,j})

\displaystyle\mapsto P:=\left(\alpha_{i,j}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{j}}{\rho_{i}}x\right)\right).

We now observe by direct computation (or reasoning on line and columns)
that, if
A\mapsto P
and if
\Gamma,\Lambda\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})
are diagonal,
then
\Gamma A\Lambda^{-1}\mapsto\Gamma P\Lambda^{-1}
.

A similar study about the right factor
Q\in F_{C}^{\prime\prime}
in the Mano decomposition
leads us to introduce the elliptic function:

\Phi_{C}^{\prime\prime}(x):=\dfrac{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\xi_{1}}x\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\xi_{2}}x\right)}{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\xi_{1}}x\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\xi_{2}}x\right)},

which is of degree
2
and such that
\Phi_{C}^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})=\Phi_{C}^{\prime\prime}(x_{4})
; and to set:

t:=\dfrac{1}{\Phi_{C}^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})}=\dfrac{1}{\Phi_{C}^{\prime\prime}(x_{4})}\in{\mathbf{C}}\cup\{\infty\}.

We then define the space of matrices:

{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}:=\{B:=(\beta_{i,j})\in{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})^{*}\mid\alpha(B)=t\}.

This yields a bijection:

\displaystyle{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\rightarrow F_{C}^{\prime\prime},

\displaystyle B:=(\beta_{i,j})

\displaystyle\mapsto Q:=\left(\beta_{i,j}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\xi_{i}}x\right)\right).

Again we observe that, if
B\mapsto Q
and if
\Lambda,\Delta\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})

are diagonal,
then
\Lambda B\Delta^{-1}\mapsto\Lambda Q\Delta^{-1}
.

Proposition 6.4

We thereby define a bijection:

\dfrac{{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\sim}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\dfrac{F_{C}^{\prime}\times F_{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\sim}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{F}}_{C},

where the equivalence relation in the left hand side is defined by

(A,B)\sim(\Gamma A\Lambda^{-1},\Lambda B\Delta^{-1})
for all

(A,B)\in{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}
and for all invertible
diagonal matrices
\Gamma,\Lambda,\Delta
. The rightmost arrow
was previously defined: it is induced by the multiplication map

F_{C}^{\prime}\times F_{C}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
,
(P,Q)\mapsto PQ
.

\Box

6.3.2
Keeping track of the involution

C={\text{Diag}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\mapsto\tilde{C}:=JCJ={\text{Diag}}(\xi_{2},\xi_{1})

Recall that
J=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
and that,
writing
\tilde{C}:=JCJ
, we have
F_{C}=F_{\tilde{C}}
by proposition

5.9
and therefore
{\mathcal{F}}_{C}={\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{C}}
. We intend to
produce a “coordinate” on
{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
by using the bijection described in
the above proposition. This is not the same if we use
C
or
\tilde{C}
:
we have to make explicit this involutive relationship.

More precisely, we have a bijection:

\displaystyle F_{C}^{\prime}\times F_{C}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\rightarrow F_{\tilde{C}}^{\prime}\times F_{\tilde{C}}^{\prime\prime},

\displaystyle(P,Q)

\displaystyle\mapsto(PJ,JQ)

yielding the left vertical map of a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{F_{C}^{\prime}\times F_{C}^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{F_{\tilde{C}}^{\prime}\times F_{\tilde{C}}^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F_{\tilde{C}}.}

Intepreting the multiplications by
J
in terms of exchanging lines or columns,
we get another commutative diagram of bijections:

\textstyle{{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F_{C}^{\prime}\times F_{C}^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F_{\tilde{C}}^{\prime}\times F_{\tilde{C}}^{\prime\prime},}

where the left vertical map is
(A,B)\mapsto(AJ,JB)
.

Write
\tilde{\Lambda}:=J\Lambda J
. From the obvious relations:

(\Gamma A\Lambda^{-1})J=\Gamma(AJ)\tilde{\Lambda}^{-1}\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }J(\Lambda B\Delta^{-1})=\tilde{\Lambda}(JB)\Delta^{-1},

we deduce that said map
(A,B)\mapsto(AJ,JB)
is compatible with the
equivalence relation on
{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}
. So in the end
we get a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\dfrac{{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\sim}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dfrac{F_{C}^{\prime}\times F_{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\sim}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{F}}_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\dfrac{{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\sim}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dfrac{F_{\tilde{C}}^{\prime}\times F_{\tilde{C}^{\prime\prime}}}{\sim}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{C}}}

6.3.3
A list of special cases

We shall describe

\dfrac{{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\sim}

by looking for normal forms for pairs
(A,B)\in{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}
.
When all coefficients of
A
and
B
are non zero, the answer is simpler, so we
first discuss here the possibility that some coefficients vanish. We shall only
analyse the case that
\alpha_{1,1}=0
; the other cases will be expounded
dogmatically, the arguments being entirely similar.

If
\alpha_{1,1}=0
, then
\alpha_{1,2}\alpha_{2,1}\neq 0
. Let
P\in F_{C}^{\prime}

the corresponding matrix; then

\det P=-\alpha_{1,2}\alpha_{2,1}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{1}}x\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)

must vanish at
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, which means that one of its theta factors
vanishes at
x_{1}
and the other at
x_{2}
. This implies that either

\xi_{1}\equiv-\dfrac{\rho_{2}}{x_{1}}
and
\xi_{2}\equiv-\dfrac{\rho_{1}}{x_{2}}
or

\xi_{1}\equiv-\dfrac{\rho_{1}}{x_{2}}
and
\xi_{2}\equiv-\dfrac{\rho_{2}}{x_{1}}
.
(We could alternatively use the fact that

\dfrac{\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}}{\alpha_{1,2}\alpha_{2,1}}=\dfrac{1}{\Phi_{C}^{\prime}(x_{1})}=\dfrac{1}{\Phi_{C}^{\prime}(x_{2})}
.) With the notations of

5.1
and
5.2
,
we also see that either
f_{2}=0
and
\Phi(\xi_{1})=\Phi(\xi_{2})=\infty
,
or
f_{1}=0
and
\Phi(\xi_{1})=\Phi(\xi_{2})=0
.

Conversely, if for instance

\xi_{1}\equiv-\dfrac{\rho_{2}}{x_{1}}
(and therefore

\xi_{2}\equiv-\dfrac{\rho_{1}}{x_{2}}
), we have

{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x_{1}\right)=0
, whence

\det P(x_{1})=\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x_{1}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x_{1}\right)=0
.
Under the assumption (NR) and due to the fact that we are in the generic case,

{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)

has no common zero with
{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x\right)
. So the above
in turn implies that
\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}=0
.

We summarize in the following table the list of all possible cases of
vanishing of some
\alpha_{i,j}
:

vanishing

class of
\xi_{1}

class of
\xi_{2}

value of one

\Phi(\xi_{1})=

coefficient

\pmod{q^{\mathbf{Z}}}

\pmod{q^{\mathbf{Z}}}

of the
f_{i}
,
g_{i}

\Phi(\xi_{2})=

\alpha_{1,1}=0

\xi_{1}\equiv-\rho_{2}/x_{1}

\xi_{2}\equiv-\rho_{1}/x_{2}

f_{2}=0

\infty

\alpha_{1,1}=0

\xi_{1}\equiv-\rho_{2}/x_{2}

\xi_{2}\equiv-\rho_{1}/x_{1}

f_{1}=0

0

\alpha_{2,2}=0

\xi_{1}\equiv-\rho_{2}/x_{1}

\xi_{2}\equiv-\rho_{1}/x_{2}

g_{1}=0

\infty

\alpha_{2,2}=0

\xi_{1}\equiv-\rho_{2}/x_{2}

\xi_{2}\equiv-\rho_{1}/x_{1}

g_{2}=0

0

\alpha_{1,2}=0

\xi_{1}\equiv-\rho_{1}/x_{1}

\xi_{2}\equiv-\rho_{2}/x_{2}

f_{1}=0

0

\alpha_{1,2}=0

\xi_{1}\equiv-\rho_{1}/x_{2}

\xi_{2}\equiv-\rho_{2}/x_{1}

f_{2}=0

\infty

\alpha_{2,1}=0

\xi_{1}\equiv-\rho_{1}/x_{1}

\xi_{2}\equiv-\rho_{2}/x_{2}

g_{2}=0

0

\alpha_{2,1}=0

\xi_{1}\equiv-\rho_{1}/x_{2}

\xi_{2}\equiv-\rho_{2}/x_{1}

g_{1}=0

\infty

Just for the few following definitions, we shall, for every
a\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
, write

R(a)
its unique representative in
{\mathcal{C}_{q}}
,
i.e.

\{R(a)\}=[a;q]\cap{\mathcal{C}_{q}}
. We
introduce the sets of special values:

\Xi^{\prime}_{1}:=\{R(-\rho_{1}/x_{1}),R(-\rho_{1}/x_{2})\},\quad\Xi^{\prime}_{2}:=\{R(-\rho_{2}/x_{1}),R(-\rho_{2}/x_{2})\},\quad\Xi^{\prime}:=\Xi^{\prime}_{1}\cup\Xi^{\prime}_{2}\subset{\mathbf{C}}^{*},

Then we can also express our conditions as:

\xi_{1}\in\Xi^{\prime}_{1}\Longleftrightarrow\xi_{2}\in\Xi^{\prime}_{2}\Longleftrightarrow\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}=0,\quad\xi_{1}\in\Xi^{\prime}_{2}\Longleftrightarrow\xi_{2}\in\Xi^{\prime}_{1}\Longleftrightarrow\alpha_{1,2}\alpha_{2,1}=0.

There are similar results for the vanishing of the
\beta_{i,j}
, but we do not
tabulate them (although they will be used when necessary). In short, all the

\beta_{i,j}
are non zero except maybe if
\xi_{1}
is congruent modulo
q^{\mathbf{Z}}

to one of the
-\sigma_{j}x_{i}
,
i=3,4
,
j=1,2
. (Here, one must take in
account vanishing of such expressions as

{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\xi_{1}}x_{i}\right)
.) So we define new sets of special
values:

\Xi^{\prime\prime}_{1}:=\{R(-\sigma_{1}x_{3}),R(-\sigma_{1}x_{4})\},\quad\Xi^{\prime\prime}_{2}:=\{R(-\sigma_{2}x_{3}),R(-\sigma_{2}x_{4})\},\quad\Xi^{\prime\prime}:=\Xi^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cup\Xi^{\prime\prime}_{2}\subset{\mathbf{C}}^{*}.

We have:

\xi_{1}\in\Xi^{\prime\prime}_{2}\Longleftrightarrow\xi_{2}\in\Xi^{\prime\prime}_{1}\Longleftrightarrow\beta_{1,1}\beta_{2,2}=0,\quad\xi_{1}\in\Xi^{\prime\prime}_{1}\Longleftrightarrow\xi_{2}\in\Xi^{\prime\prime}_{2}\Longleftrightarrow\beta_{1,2}\beta_{2,1}=0.

Altogether we get the following set of special values:

\Xi:=\Xi^{\prime}\cup\Xi^{\prime\prime}.

From now on, we shall assume for simplicity that these eight special values (
i.e.
their classes in
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
) are pairwise distinct:

(6.4.1)

\text{Assumption\penalty\ }\mathbf{Hyp}_{8}:\quad{\text{card}}\ \Xi=8.

Besides, this implies that
\xi_{1}\not\equiv\xi_{2}
for all
\xi_{1}\in\Xi_{0}
,
so all the “general values” in
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\setminus\Xi_{0}
actually fall under
the generic case being presently studied.

6.3.4
General fiber

We assume here that
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\not\in\Xi
. Then, for all pairs

(A,B)\in{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}
, all the coefficients

\alpha_{i,j},\beta_{i,j}
,
i,j=1,2
are non zero.

Straightforward use of the action of
\Gamma,\Lambda
allows one to bring
A

to the form
\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&?\end{pmatrix}
and the missing
coefficient is necessarily
s
. Then the only possible actions of
\Gamma
,

\Lambda
preserving that form are those such that

\gamma_{1}/\lambda_{1}=\gamma_{1}/\lambda_{2}=\gamma_{2}/\lambda_{1}=1
, whence

\Gamma=\Lambda=\lambda I_{2}
for some
\lambda\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
.This means that
we can only act on
B
, while preserving the normal form for
A
, by maps

B\mapsto\lambda B\Delta^{-1}
. This allows one to bring
B
to the form

\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
\eta&y\end{pmatrix}
with the relation
y=t\eta
.
In the end, we obtain a normal form for
(A,B)
:

(A,B)\sim(A_{0},D_{\eta}B_{0}),\text{\penalty\ where\penalty\ }A_{0}:=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&s\end{pmatrix},\quad B_{0}:=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&t\end{pmatrix},\quad D_{\eta}:=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\eta\end{pmatrix}.

Here
s,t
are fixed by the value of the base point
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in X
but

x\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
is the one free parameter (indeed, a coordinate) characterizing
the class of
(A,B)
.

The action of the involution permutes the columns of
A
and the lines of
B
.
It sends
(A_{0},D_{\eta}B_{0})
to:

(A_{0}J,JD_{\eta}B_{0})=\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
s&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\eta&\eta t\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}\right)\sim\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&s^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
\eta^{-1}&\eta^{-1}t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\right),

the equivalence being induced by the action of:

(\Gamma,\Lambda,\Delta):=\left({\text{Diag}}(1,s^{-1}),I_{2},{\text{Diag}}(\eta,\eta t)\right).

Note that here we consider the fiber over
\tilde{C}={\text{Diag}}(\xi_{2},\xi_{1})

and it is readily checked that
s
and
t
must respectively be replaced
by
s^{-1}
and
t^{-1}
. We conclude that the coordinate
\eta
must correspondingly
be replaced by
\eta^{-1}
.

6.3.5
Special fibers

Assume for instance that
\xi_{1}\equiv-\rho_{1}/x_{1}
(the other possibilities
are entirely similar). Let
(A,B)\in{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}
. Then
all the coefficients
\beta_{i,j}
,
i,j=1,2
as well as
\alpha_{1,1}
and

\alpha_{2,2}
are non zero; but
\alpha_{1,2}\alpha_{2,1}=0
(see the table
in
6.3.3
).

If
\alpha_{1,2}=0\neq\alpha_{2,1}
, use of
\Gamma,\Lambda
allows one to
reduce
A
to the form
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}
. This
pattern can only be preserved by further transformations such that

\Gamma=\Lambda=\lambda I_{2}
, so possible transformations of
B
must have
the form
B\mapsto\lambda B\Delta^{-1}
. This allows one to bring
B
to the
form
D_{\eta}B_{0}
as before. So pairs
(A,B)
of this type give rise to one line
in
{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
, parameterized by
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
. The case
\alpha_{1,2}\neq 0=\alpha_{2,1}

is obviously similar and leads to the same conclusion.

Now, if
\alpha_{1,2}=\alpha_{2,1}=0
,
A
can be brought to the form
I_{2}
but
all pairs
(\Gamma,\Lambda)
such that
\Gamma=\Lambda
preserve that form, so
that all actions
B\mapsto\Lambda B\Delta^{-1}
are allowed and
B
can be
brought to the form
B_{0}
. This means that all these pairs define a unique point
in
{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
. Since this point is a degeneracy of each of the two punctured
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}

lines found above, the resulting figure (for the special fiber over

\xi_{1}\equiv-\rho_{1}/x_{1}
or for any of the other possibilities) is: two
{\mathbf{C}}

lines intersecting at a point.

Now we discuss the involution. It replaces
\xi_{1}\equiv-\rho_{1}/x_{1}
by

\xi_{1}\equiv-\rho_{2}/x_{2}
and the reduced pair

\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},D_{\eta}B_{0}\right)
by

\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},JD_{\eta}B_{0}\right)
,
which is equivalent to

\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
\eta^{-1}&\eta^{-1}t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\right)
.
So the two
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
-lines above
-\rho_{1}/x_{1}
go isomorphically to the two special
lines above
-\rho_{2}/x_{2}
; and obviously, the degenerate point to the degenerate
point. Therefore, after going to the quotient by the involution, each special
fiber still consists in two
{\mathbf{C}}
lines intersecting at a point.

6.4
Description of
{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
in the logarithmic case

Let
C:=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\xi\\
0&\xi\end{pmatrix}
, where

\xi\in{\mathcal{C}_{q}}
and
\xi^{2}\equiv\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}\cdot

Matrices
\Lambda\in{\text{GL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})
commuting with
C
are those of the form

\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&\mu\\
0&\lambda\end{pmatrix}
,
where
\lambda\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
and
\mu\in{\mathbf{C}}
.

From
5.3.1
, we know that corresponding left
factors in Mano decomposition have the form

P=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1,1}\phi_{1}&\alpha_{1,1}\psi_{1}+\alpha_{1,2}\phi_{1}\\
\alpha_{2,1}\phi_{2}&\alpha_{2,1}\psi_{2}+\alpha_{2,2}\phi_{2}\end{pmatrix}

for some
\alpha_{i,j}\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
i,j=1,2
, where

\phi_{i}(x):={\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{i}}x\right)
and

\psi_{i}(x):=x\phi_{i}^{\prime}(x)=\frac{\xi}{\rho_{i}}x{\theta_{q}^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{i}}x\right)
,
so that:

\det P=(\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}-\alpha_{2,1}\alpha_{1,2})\phi_{1}\phi_{2}+\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,1}x(\phi_{1}\phi_{2}^{\prime}-\phi_{2}\phi_{1}^{\prime}).

Also condition
\det P\neq 0
is equivalent to

(\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,1},\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}-\alpha_{2,1}\alpha_{1,2})\neq(0,0)
.

We note that
\phi_{1}\phi_{2}(x_{1})\neq 0
. Indeed, if for instance

\phi_{1}(x_{1})=0
, then
\xi\equiv-\rho_{1}/x_{1}
, so
\xi\in\Xi^{\prime}

which we saw was impossible. (Direct argument: the congruence property
on
\xi^{2}
would imply
\xi\equiv-\rho_{2}/x_{2}
, whence

\rho_{1}/x_{1}\equiv\rho_{2}/x_{2}
, contrary to the assumption that
\Xi

has eight pairwise distinct elements.)

We also deduce that
\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,1}\neq 0
because otherwise
the condition
\det\ P(x_{1})=0
would imply

\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}-\alpha_{2,1}=0
, then

(\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,1},\alpha_{1,1}\alpha_{2,2}-\alpha_{2,1}\alpha_{1,2})=(0,0)
. So in fact a small calculation yields:

\det\ P(x_{1})=0\Longleftrightarrow\dfrac{\alpha_{2,2}}{\alpha_{2,1}}-\dfrac{\alpha_{1,2}}{\alpha_{1,1}}=u,\text{\penalty\ where\penalty\ }u:=x_{1}\left(\dfrac{\phi_{1}^{\prime}}{\phi_{1}}-\dfrac{\phi_{2}^{\prime}}{\phi_{2}}\right)(x_{1}).

So here we introduce the space of matrices:

{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}:=\left\{A:=(\alpha_{i,j})\in{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})\Bigm|\begin{cases}\alpha_{1,1},\alpha_{2,2}\neq 0,\\
\dfrac{\alpha_{2,2}}{\alpha_{2,1}}-\dfrac{\alpha_{1,2}}{\alpha_{1,1}}=u.\end{cases}\right\}.

An easy calculation shows that action
P\mapsto\Gamma P\Lambda^{-1}
,
with diagonal
\Gamma
and
\Lambda
as shown above, translates to the
similar action on
A
.

We now go into the corresponding calculations for the right factors
Q

in Mano decomposition; they have the form

Q=\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{1,1}\overline{\phi}_{1}-\beta_{2,1}\overline{\psi}_{1}&\beta_{1,2}\overline{\phi}_{2}-\beta_{2,2}\overline{\psi}_{2}\\
\beta_{2,1}\overline{\phi}_{1}&\beta_{2,2}\overline{\phi}_{2}\end{pmatrix}

for some
\beta_{i,j}\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
i,j=1,2
, where

\overline{\phi}_{j}(x):={\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\xi}x\right)
and

\overline{\psi}_{j}(x):=x\overline{\phi}_{j}^{\prime}(x)
,
so that:

\det Q=(\beta_{1,1}\beta_{2,2}-\beta_{2,1}\beta_{1,2})\overline{\phi}_{1}\overline{\phi}_{2}+\beta_{2,1}\beta_{2,2}\,x(\overline{\phi}_{1}\overline{\phi}_{2}^{\prime}-\overline{\phi}_{2}\overline{\phi}_{1}^{\prime}).

In the same way as before, we are led to set:

v:=x_{3}\left(\dfrac{\overline{\phi}_{1}^{\prime}}{\overline{\phi}_{1}}-\dfrac{\overline{\phi}_{2}^{\prime}}{\overline{\phi}_{2}}\right)(x_{3})

and to define:

{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}:=\left\{B:=(\beta_{i,j})\in{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})\Bigm|\begin{cases}\beta_{1,1},\beta_{2,2}\neq 0,\\
\dfrac{\beta_{2,2}}{\beta_{1,2}}-\dfrac{\beta_{2,1}}{\beta_{1,1}}=v.\end{cases}\right\}.

Again we find that action
Q\mapsto\Lambda Q\Delta^{-1}
, with diagonal

\Delta
and
\Lambda
as shown above, translates to the similar action
on
B
. So we get a bijection:

\dfrac{{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}}{\sim}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{F}}_{C},

where relation
\sim
on
{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}
is defined by
the action

(A,B)\mapsto\left(\Gamma A\Lambda^{-1},\Lambda B\Delta^{-1}\right)

of triples
(\Gamma,\Lambda,\Delta)
, where
\Gamma,\Delta
are diagonal
invertible and

\Lambda:=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&\mu\\
0&\lambda\end{pmatrix}
,
for some
\lambda\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
and
\mu\in{\mathbf{C}}
. It is easily checked that
this action indeed sends
{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime\prime}
to itself.

Action of
\Gamma
can be used to reduce
A
to the form

\begin{pmatrix}1&?\\
1&?\end{pmatrix}
, then action of
\Lambda

to the form
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&u\end{pmatrix}
, where the
down right coefficient
u
is forced upon us by the condition defining

{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})_{C}^{\prime}
. Then the only possibility to preserve this form is to have

\Gamma=\Lambda
, a scalar matrix. So the remaining possible actions
on
B
are
B\mapsto\lambda B\Delta^{-1}
,
\lambda\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
and

\Delta
diagonal invertible. This can be used to reduce
B
to the form

\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
x&y\end{pmatrix}
with condition
y-x=v
.
So the mapping:

x\mapsto\text{\penalty\ the class of\penalty\ }\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
x&x+v\end{pmatrix}

induces a bijective parameterisation of
{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
by
{\mathbf{C}}
.

6.5
Putting it all together

Recall our assumptions from the beginning of section
6
:
Fuchs relation (FR), strong non resonancy (NR) and non splitting (NS); to which
we added in
6.3.3
assumption
\mathbf{Hyp}_{8}
.

There are three components in
{\mathcal{F}}
, two of which project to finite subsets
of the base:

1.

The logarithmic part
\bigcup\limits_{C\in N_{u}}{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
is in bijection with

\Upsilon\times{\mathbf{C}}
, where
\Upsilon
is the set of square roots of

\pi\left(\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}\right)=\pi(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}x_{3}x_{4})

in
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
(so
{\text{card}}\ \Upsilon=4
). We simplify the formulation by saying that
“the logarithmic part is
\Upsilon\times{\mathbf{C}}
”, and similarly for the following.

2.

Putting together the special fibers in the generic part and identifying

\Xi
by its image in
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
, we have (set theoretically) the quotient of

\Xi\times({\mathbf{C}}^{*}\sqcup{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\sqcup\{.\})
by the involution. Choosing
a representative subset
\Xi_{0}
of
\Xi
for the involution

\xi_{1}\leftrightarrow\xi_{2}
(thus
{\text{card}}\ \Xi_{0}=4
), we see that this
quotient can be identified with
\Xi_{0}\times({\mathbf{C}}^{*}\sqcup{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\sqcup\{.\})
.

3.

Putting together the general fibers, we have (again set theoretically)
the quotient of
\left({\mathbf{E}_{q}}\setminus(\Xi\cup\Upsilon)\right)\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}

by the involution. We shall give a closer look at this component in

6.5.3
.

6.5.1
The fibering: generic part

We must justify our contention that
\Pi:{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
is a fibration
and that its general (non logarithmic) part has exactly four special fibers.
So we set
{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{\bullet}}:={\mathbf{E}_{q}}\setminus\Upsilon
and
{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})^{\bullet}}
its image under
\Phi
,
a projective line minus four points (actually the critical values of
\Phi
),
so that the restriction
\Phi:{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{\bullet}}\rightarrow{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})^{\bullet}}
is an
unramified

degree
2
covering. We write
{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\bullet}}
the subset of
{\mathcal{C}_{q}}
corresponding to

{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{\bullet}}
, so that
\pi
induces a bijection
{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\bullet}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{\bullet}}
.

For
\xi\in{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\bullet}}
, we write
\tilde{\xi}\in{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\bullet}}
its image under the
involution,
i.e.
the unique element of
{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\bullet}}
such that

\xi\tilde{\xi}\equiv\rho_{1}\rho_{2}/(x_{1}x_{2})=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}x_{3}x_{4}
;
and
C(\xi):={\text{Diag}}\left(\xi,\tilde{\xi}\right)
. Accordingly, we write

F_{\xi}:=F_{C(\xi)}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi}:={\mathcal{F}}_{C(\xi)}
.

Last, in order to have a unified picture, we set:

\Phi^{\prime}(\xi):=\Phi^{\prime}_{C(\xi)}(x_{1})=\Phi^{\prime}_{C(\xi)}(x_{2})=\dfrac{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{1}}x_{1}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\tilde{\xi}}{\rho_{2}}x_{1}\right)}{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\tilde{\xi}}{\rho_{1}}x_{1}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{2}}x_{1}\right)}=\dfrac{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{1}}x_{1}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\rho_{1}}{x_{2}\xi}\right)}{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\rho_{2}}x_{1}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\rho_{2}}{x_{2}\xi}\right)}=\dfrac{\rho_{1}}{\rho_{2}}\;\dfrac{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{x_{1}}{\rho_{1}}\xi\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{x_{2}}{\rho_{1}}\xi\right)}{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{x_{1}}{\rho_{2}}\xi\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{x_{2}}{\rho_{2}}\xi\right)}

and

\Phi^{\prime\prime}(\xi):=\Phi_{C(\xi)}^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})=\Phi_{C(\xi)}^{\prime\prime}(x_{4})=\dfrac{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\xi}x_{3}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\tilde{\xi}}x_{3}\right)}{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\xi}x_{3}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\tilde{\xi}}x_{3}\right)}=\dfrac{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\xi}x_{3}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{1}x_{4}}\right)}{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\xi}x_{3}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{2}x_{4}}\right)}=\dfrac{\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{2}}\;\dfrac{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{1}x_{3}}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{1}x_{4}}\right)}{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{2}x_{3}}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{2}x_{4}}\right)}\cdot

(We used the Fuchs relation and the functional equation
