{\theta_{q}}(1/x)=(1/x){\theta_{q}}(x)
,
see
2.3
, page
2.3
.)

After
6.3
, we have a cartesian square:

\textstyle{{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})^{*}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha\times\alpha}
\textstyle{\underset{\xi\in{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\bullet}}}{\bigsqcup}F_{\xi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})^{\bullet}}\times{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})^{\bullet}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\bullet}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt(1/\Phi^{\prime},1/\Phi^{\prime\prime})}

Taking in account the
(\Gamma,\Lambda,\Delta)
action, this gives rise
to a bigger commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\dfrac{{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})^{*}\times{\text{Mat}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})^{*}}{(\Gamma,\Lambda,\Delta)-\text{action}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha\times\alpha}
\textstyle{\underset{\xi\in{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\bullet}}}{\bigsqcup}{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
quotient by involution
\textstyle{{\mathcal{F}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi}
\textstyle{{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})^{\bullet}}\times{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})^{\bullet}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\bullet}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt(1/\Phi^{\prime},1/\Phi^{\prime\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}

The left hand square is cartesian, the right hand square is only commutative.

Now it follows that the fibers in the generic part have the following form:

\Pi^{-1}(\Phi(\xi))=\dfrac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\xi}\sqcup{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}{\text{involution}},

and the case-by-case computation in
6.3
says that
the fiber is degenerate (“special case”) if, and only if

\Phi^{\prime}(\xi)\in\{0,\infty\}
or
\Phi^{\prime\prime}(\xi)\in\{0,\infty\}
, that is, after
the above computations, if
\xi\in\Xi^{\prime}
or if
\xi\in\Xi^{\prime\prime}
respectively.
Taking in account the involution
\xi\leftrightarrow\tilde{\xi}
, for which
each of
\Xi^{\prime}
,
\Xi^{\prime\prime}
is invariant, this means that there are
four

critical values in
{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})^{\bullet}}
giving rise to special fibers
\Pi^{-1}(-)
of the
form
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\sqcup{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\sqcup\{.\}
:

\displaystyle\Phi\left(-\rho_{1}/x_{1}\right)=\Phi\left(-\rho_{2}/x_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\Phi\left(-\rho_{1}/x_{2}\right)=\Phi\left(-\rho_{2}/x_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\infty,

\displaystyle\Phi\left(-\sigma_{1}x_{3}\right)=\Phi\left(-\sigma_{2}x_{4}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x_{1}x_{3}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x_{2}x_{3}\right)}{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x_{2}x_{3}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x_{1}x_{3}\right)},

\displaystyle\Phi\left(-\sigma_{1}x_{4}\right)=\Phi\left(-\sigma_{2}x_{3}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\rho_{1}}x_{1}x_{3}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x_{2}x_{3}\right)}{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\rho_{1}}x_{2}x_{3}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x_{1}x_{3}\right)}\cdot

Other expressions are possible for the last two critical values, but we
found no simple ones.

6.5.2
More about special fibers

So special fibers correspond to the vanishing of one of the
\alpha_{i,j}
or one
of the
\beta_{i,j}
; with for instance the conditions
\alpha_{1,1}=0
and

\alpha_{2,2}=0
each providing a line of the same special fiber (and of course
these two lines intersect where
\alpha_{1,1}=\alpha_{2,2}=0
).

The first two of the above four special fibers were already met when analyzing
the behaviour of
\Pi
in
5.1.2
. Indeed, when
encoding the factors
P
and
Q
of the Mano decomposition
M=PQ
by matrices

A:=(\alpha_{i,j})
and
B:=(\beta_{i,j})
, we find that one of the
\alpha_{i,j}

is zero if, and only if one coefficient of
P
vanishes and this, by already
explained arguments, is equivalent to:
M(x_{1})
or
M(x_{2})
has a null line.
We already saw in
5.1.1
that this is
equivalent to one (at least) of
f_{1},f_{2},g_{1},g_{2}
vanishes,
i.e.
to

\Pi(M)\in\{0,\infty\}
. And actually each of the four lines found in

6.3.5
corresponds to one of those conditions.

This means that
M
belonging to one of the lines of one of the first two special
fibers can be read either on
M(x_{1})
or on
M(x_{2})
, independently of each other.
So if we refine our notation and write
\Pi_{1,2}
for the above
\Pi
and more
generally
\Pi_{i,j}
for the one obtaining by using
x_{i},x_{j}
instead of
x_{1},x_{2}
,
we see that each of these four lines is a line of one of the first two special
fibers of
\Pi_{1,3}
or
\Pi_{1,4}
or
\Pi_{2,3}
or
\Pi_{2,4}
.

Now we are going to see that the last two of the four special fibers can be read
on
M(x_{3})
and
M(x_{4})
, although not on
\Pi_{3,4}
. Actually, the same line of
argument as above leads to: one of the
\beta_{i,j}
is zero if, and only if

M(x_{3})
or
M(x_{4})
has a zero
column
. This leads us to introduce for each
of them (both have rank
1
) one non zero
line
, say
(u_{3},v_{3})
and

(u_{4},v_{4})
; and to define:

\Pi^{\prime}_{3,4}(M):=\dfrac{u_{3}v_{4}}{u_{4}v_{3}}\in{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}.

Then we conclude from the above discussion that
M
is in one of the last two
special fibers if, and only if
\Pi^{\prime}_{3,4}(M)\in\{0,\infty\}
.

Remark 6.5

The relation of the
\Pi_{i,j}
and
\Pi^{\prime}_{i,j}
projective invariants is subtle
and interesting in its own right. Matrices
M(x_{i})
have rank one, thus can be
written
C_{i}\times L_{i}
, a product of a column by a line matrix, both non zero
and defined up to a non zero scalar factor. We saw in lemma

5.4
of
5.1.1

that generically
\Pi_{i,j}
is a complete invariant for the left action of
diagonal matrices on pairs
(C_{i},C_{j})
. Clearly,
\Pi^{\prime}_{i,j}
is a complete
invariant for the right action of diagonal matrices on pairs
(L_{i},L_{j})
.

6.5.3
Algebro-geometric description of the general component

The space of interest is the quotient of

\left({\mathbf{E}_{q}}\setminus(\Xi\cup\Upsilon)\right)\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
by the
the involution
(\overline{\xi_{1}},\eta)\mapsto(\overline{\xi_{2}},\eta^{-1})
,
where
\overline{\xi_{1}}\overline{\xi_{2}}=a
, the particular class
written above (recall that we write multiplicatively the group law
in
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
). We extend this involution to
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
. Also after
choosing a particular square root
\alpha
of
a
, we can instead
use a parameter
t\in{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
such that
\overline{\xi_{1}}=\alpha t

and
\overline{\xi_{2}}=\alpha t^{-1}
. So we must find the quotient
of
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
by the involution
\tau:(t,\eta)\mapsto(t^{-1},\eta^{-1})
.

In the usual projective model of
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
, the point at infinity is its
own inverse. So it makes sense to restrict the involution to the affine
algebraic set
{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{*}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
, where
{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{*}}:={\mathbf{E}_{q}}\setminus\{\infty\}
.
For the latter we have an algebro-geometric model:

{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{*}}=\text{Spec}\ {\mathbf{C}}[x,y]\text{\penalty\ where\penalty\ }{\mathbf{C}}[x,y]:=\dfrac{{\mathbf{C}}[X,Y]}{Y^{2}-f(X)},

for some separable cubic polynomial
f(X)
. The inversion map on
{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{*}}

is dual to the automorphism of
{\mathbf{C}}[x,y]
defined by
y\mapsto-y
. In
this model (the indeterminate
z
denoting a “coordinate” for
\eta
):

{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{*}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}=\text{Spec}\ {\mathbf{C}}[x,y][z,1/z]

and the involution is dual to the automorphism of
{\mathbf{C}}[x,y][z,1/z]

defined by
y\mapsto-y
,
z\mapsto 1/z
.

The quotient of a complex affine algebraic variety by a finite group
(here the group generated by the involution) is obtained by computing
its affine algebra as the subalgebra fixed by the dual action of the
group. So:

\dfrac{{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{*}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}}{\text{involution\penalty\ }\tau}=\text{Spec}\ {\mathbf{C}}[x,y][z,1/z]^{\tau}.

We proceed to compute the invariant subalgebra
{\mathbf{C}}[x,y][z,1/z]^{\tau}
.
An element of
{\mathbf{C}}[x,y][z,1/z]
can be uniquely written as

g=\sum_{n\in{\mathbf{Z}}}a_{n}(x)z^{n}+y\sum_{n\in{\mathbf{Z}}}b_{n}(x)z^{n},\text{\penalty\ where all the\penalty\ }a_{n},b_{n}\in{\mathbf{C}}[X].

Invariance by
\tau
translates into:

g=a_{0}(x)+\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{n}(x)(z^{n}+z^{-n})+y\sum_{n\geq 1}b_{n}(x)(z^{n}-z^{-n}).

Setting
w:=\dfrac{z+z^{-1}}{2}
and
v:=\dfrac{z-z^{-1}}{2}y
,
we get the form:

g=A(x,w)+vB(x,w).

Clearly,
x
and
w
are algebraically independent and
v\not\in{\mathbf{C}}[x,w]
.
Also:

v^{2}=\dfrac{(z-z^{-1})^{2}}{4}y^{2}=(w^{2}-1)f(x).

This describes an algebraic surface, a degree
2
covering of the plane

\text{Spec}\ {\mathbf{C}}[x,w]
ramified over the set of equation
(w^{2}-1)f(x)=0

(a union of six lines).

Since for any fixed
v_{0}\neq\pm 1
the equation
w^{2}=f(x)(v_{0}^{2}-1)

defines an affine elliptic curve, our surface is also a pencil of elliptic
curves parameterized by
{\mathbf{C}}\setminus\{+1,-1\}
.

To recover the
projective
picture from the above
affine
one,
just note that each fixed point
\alpha
of the involution,
i.e.
each

\alpha=\overline{\xi}
with
\xi^{2}\equiv\dfrac{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}
,
gives rise to such an affine chart. This will be done in some detail in section

7
. Herebelow we attempt at a geometric
description of the projective surface.

6.6
Geometric description of the whole of
{\mathcal{F}}

6.6.1
Definitions

We recall the classical definitions of an
elliptic surface
.
The first one
[
4
]
is the following.

Definition 6.6

Let
S
be a complex projective surface. We will say that it is an elliptic surface
if there exists a smooth curve
B
and a surjective morphism
p:S\rightarrow B
whose
generic fiber is an elliptic curve.

In fact a variant of this definition is better for our purposes
[
73
]

(definition 3.1, page 7).

Definition 6.7

An elliptic surface
S
over
B
is a smooth projective surface S
with an elliptic fibration over
B
, i.e. a surjective morphism

p:S\rightarrow B
, such that :

(i)

almost all fibers are smooth curves of genus
1
;

(ii)

no fiber contains an exceptional curve of the first kind.

It is better to say “smooth curves of genus
1
” than “elliptic curve”. An elliptic
curve is a smooth curve of genus
1
with a marked point and definition

6.6
could suggest that there exists a section.

We recall that an exceptional curve of the first kind is a smooth rational curve of
self-intersection
-1

(
also called
(-1)
-curve
)
. Naturally,
(-1)
-curves
occur as exceptional divisors of blow-ups of surfaces at smooth points. One can
always successively blow-down
(-1)
-curves to reduce to a smooth minimal model.
Therefore an elliptic fibration in the sense of the first definition
6.6

can be transformed by a succession of blow-downs into an elliptic fibration in the
sense of the second definition
6.7
.

A
section
of an elliptic surface
p:S\rightarrow B
is a morphism

s:B\rightarrow S
such that
p\circ s={\text{Id}}_{B}
. An elliptic surface
does not necessarily admit a section.

6.6.2
An elliptic fibration. Algebraic charts on
{\mathcal{F}}

We set
\mathcal{Y}:=\dfrac{{\mathbf{E}_{q}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}}{\text{involution\penalty\ }\tau}
.
(recall that the involution
\tau
was defined at the beginning of

6.5.3
).
We have two maps
p:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
and

\Psi:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})
: the maps induced respectively by

p:(\xi,\eta)\mapsto w:=\frac{1}{2}(\eta+1/\eta)\quad\text{and}\quad\Psi:(\xi,\eta)\mapsto\Phi(\xi).

—

The map
p
gives an elliptic fibration of
\mathcal{Y}
with two exceptionnal
fibers above
\pm 1
.

—

For
\eta\neq\pm 1
, the canonical map
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}\times\{\eta\}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}

induces an isomorphism of
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
onto the generic fiber

p^{-1}(w)

(
w=\frac{1}{2}(\eta+\eta^{-1})
)
.

—

The map
\Psi
induces an isomorphism between the exceptional fiber
p^{-1}(1)

(
resp.
p^{-1}(-1)
)
and
{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
.

—

For
u\in\Phi(\Upsilon)\subset{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
, the fiber
\Psi^{-1}(u)
is parameterized
bijectively by
w\in{\mathbf{C}}
. We get
4
sections of the elliptic fibration
p
.

—

For
u\in{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}\setminus\Phi(\Upsilon)
we can describe the fiber
\Psi^{-1}(u)

as two copies of
{\mathbf{C}}
glued at the points
1
and
-1
on each copy.

The surface
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
is smooth, therefore if
a\in\mathcal{Y}
is not the image
of a
fixed point
of the involution
\tau
, then
\mathcal{Y}
is smooth at
a
.
There are
8
fixed points:
\Upsilon\times\{\pm 1\}
. Their images belong to the union
of the
4
logarithmic fibers and to the union of the two exceptional fibers

p^{-1}(\pm 1)
. One verifies that these images are isolated singular
points
48
48

48

They are rational double points.
of
\mathcal{Y}
.

We can extend
\tau
into an involution
\tilde{\tau}
on
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}\times{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
.
We set
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}:=\dfrac{{\mathbf{E}_{q}}\times{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}}{\text{involution\penalty\ }\tilde{\tau}}

and we extend the elliptic fibration
p
into an elliptic fibration

\tilde{p}:\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}\rightarrow{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
. We extend
\Psi
into

\widetilde{\Psi}
.

If we remove from
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
the set
\Upsilon
, that is the
4
fixed points of the
involution
\tau:\xi\mapsto\rho_{1}\rho_{2}/x_{1}x_{2}\xi
, we get

{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{\bullet}}={\mathbf{E}_{q}}\setminus\Upsilon
. If we remove from
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
the
8
points of
\Xi
,
we get
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}^{{\dagger}}:={\mathbf{E}_{q}}\setminus\Xi
. We set
{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{\bullet}}^{\dagger}:={\mathbf{E}_{q}^{\bullet}}\cap{\mathbf{E}_{q}}^{{\dagger}}
.
We recall that the points of
\Xi
are not fixed by
\tau
, therefore we have
removed
12
points. The surfaces
{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{\bullet}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
,
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}^{\dagger}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
and

{\mathbf{E}_{q}}^{\bullet{\dagger}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
are invariant by
\tau
.

We set :

\mathcal{Y}^{\bullet}:=\dfrac{{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{\bullet}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}}{\text{involution\penalty\ }\tau},\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{Y}^{{\dagger}}:=\dfrac{{\mathbf{E}_{q}}^{\dagger}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}}{\text{involution\penalty\ }\tau},\penalty\ \mathcal{Y}^{\bullet{\dagger}}:=\dfrac{{\mathbf{E}_{q}^{\bullet}}^{\dagger}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}}{\text{involution\penalty\ }\tau}\cdot

We have some “punctured elliptic fibrations” :

p^{\bullet}:\mathcal{Y}^{\bullet}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}},\penalty\ \penalty\ p^{{\dagger}}:\mathcal{Y}^{{\dagger}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}},\penalty\ \penalty\ p^{\bullet{\dagger}}:\mathcal{Y}^{\bullet{\dagger}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}

induced (by restriction) by the elliptic fibration

p:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
.

We recall that we have an injective analytic map

\psi:\mathcal{Y}^{\bullet{\dagger}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}
. Considering the algebraic structure
on
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
and the
3
affine charts described in the preceding section, we can
interpret this map (in
3
different ways) as an
algebraic chart
of the
surface
{\mathcal{F}}
. The image of this chart misses
12
lines of
{\mathcal{F}}
.

6.6.3
Description of some fibers of
\Pi

If we remove from
{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
the set
\Pi(\Upsilon)
(that is the images of the
4

“logarithmic fibers”) we get
{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}^{\bullet}={{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}\setminus\Pi(\Upsilon)
. If we
remove from
{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
the
4
points of
\Pi(\Xi)
(that is
0
,
\infty
and two
other points), we get
{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}^{{\dagger}}:={{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}\setminus\Pi(\Xi)
. We set

{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}^{\bullet{\dagger}}:={{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}^{\bullet}\cap{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}^{\dagger}
: we have removed
8
points.

Using the elliptic fibration
p
we can describe the fibers of
\Pi
above

{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}^{\bullet{\dagger}}
(the generic fibers).

Let
u\in{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}^{\bullet{\dagger}}
. Then
\psi
is defined and we have
\Pi\circ\psi=\Psi
,
hence
\psi
induces an isomorphism of
\Psi^{-1}(u)
onto
\Pi^{-1}(u)
. Therefore
we can describe the algebraic curve
\Pi^{-1}(u)
as two copies of
{\mathbf{C}}
glued at
the points
1
and
-1
on each copy
49
49

49

A model is given by two parabolas
in
{\mathbf{C}}^{2}
in general position.
.

The open set
\Psi^{-1}\left({\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})^{\bullet}\right)=\mathcal{Y}^{\bullet}
is smooth
(it does not contain singular points). A fortiori

\Psi^{-1}\left({\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})^{\bullet{\dagger}}\right)=\mathcal{Y}^{\bullet{\dagger}}
is smooth and

\psi(\mathcal{Y}^{\bullet{\dagger}})=\Pi^{-1}\left({\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})^{\bullet{\dagger}}\right)\subset{\mathcal{F}}

is also smooth. We conjecture that
{\mathcal{F}}
is also smooth in a convenient neighborhood
of each logarithmic fiber. (We will return to this question later,
cf.

7.2.3
).

Conjecture 6.8

The inverse image
\Pi^{-1}\left({\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})^{{\dagger}}\right)\subset{\mathcal{F}}
is smooth.

Otherwise speaking we can add to
\psi(\mathcal{Y}^{\bullet{\dagger}})

the
4
logarithmic fibers and we get a smooth open subset of
{\mathcal{F}}
.

When
u\in{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})^{\bullet{\dagger}}
tends to

u_{0}\in\Pi((\Upsilon))
, the generic fiber tends is some sense towards a logarithmic
fiber (
cf.

7.2.3
). In order to prove the conjecture it seems necessary to
understand more precisely what happens. Blow ups of the
8
singular points of

\mathcal{Y}
could be useful (
cf.

7.2.3
).

The fibers above
u\in\Pi(\Xi)
are made of two affine lines intersecting at
one point (for
u=0
and
u=\infty
it is the theorem
5.5
,
for the two other values , see
6.3.5
).

It is difficult to describe what happens to the generic fibers
\Pi^{-1}(u)
when

u\in{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}^{\bullet{\dagger}}
tends to
u_{1}\in\Pi(\Xi)
. It seems that the two points

\Pi^{-1}(u)\cap p^{-1}(1)
and the two points
\Pi^{-1}(u)\cap p^{-1}(-1)
glue into
an unique point: the special point of
\Pi^{-1}(u_{1})
which is the intersection of
the two affine lines.

A possible approach for a description is to reparameterize
{\mathbf{C}}\setminus\{\pm 1\}

using an affine transform of
{\mathbf{C}}
sending
\pm 1
to
\pm\,\epsilon
. We get a
family of elliptic fibrations above the family of punctured lines

\left\{{\mathbf{C}}\setminus\{\pm\,\epsilon\}\right\}_{\epsilon}
. Then we can try to describe
what happens when
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. A similar method works perfectly for a
description of the fibration of the cubic surface
\mathcal{S}_{VI}
analog to the
fibration by
\Pi
:
cf.
below
—
‣
7.1.3

“A simple model”
.

6.6.4
An heuristic description of the fibration by
\Pi

For a generic
u\in{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
, we can describe the algebraic curve

\widetilde{\Psi}^{-1}(u)
as two copies of the projective line
{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
glued at
the points
1
and
-1
on each copy. We will give another description of the
abstract algebraic curve
\widetilde{\Psi}^{-1}(u)
(with its fibration induced
by
p
) as an algebraic curve of
\left({{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}\right)^{2}
(with the fibration induced
by the projection on the first factor). Using this picture we will give herebelow
a simple
heuristic
description of the fibration of
{\mathcal{F}}
by
\Pi
as a family
of curves.

Let
\widetilde{W}
be a
(2,2)
curve of
\left({{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}\right)^{2}
decomposed into
two
(1,1)
curves bitangent at two distinct points
A^{+}
and
A^{-}
. We denote

\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}
the projection of
\widetilde{W}
on
{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
induced by
restriction of the projection on the first factor
\left({{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}\right)^{2}\rightarrow{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
.
Up to a Möbius transform on the first factor, we can suppose that

\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}(A^{\pm})=\pm\epsilon
(
\epsilon\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
). The fibers of

\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}
above a generic point

\mathbf{w}\in{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}\setminus\{\pm\,\epsilon\}
are sets made of two points.
The special fibers above
\pm\,\epsilon
are one point sets.

For
u\in{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}^{\bullet{\dagger}}
, the two fibrations of abstract algebraic curves

\tilde{p}:\overline{\Pi^{-1}(u)}\rightarrow{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
and

\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}:\widetilde{W}\rightarrow{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}
are isomorphic:

{\mathbf{C}}\setminus\{\pm 1\}
is send bijectively to

{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}\setminus\{\pm\,\epsilon\}
. (We leave the verification to the reader.)

We set
W:=\widetilde{W}\setminus\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}^{-1}(\infty)
.
Then the two algebraic fibrations
p:\Pi^{-1}(u)\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
and

\mathbf{p}:W\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
are isomorphic.

We consider an algebraic family of fibered curves

\left(\widetilde{W}_{\lambda},\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}_{\lambda},{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}\right)_{\lambda\in{{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}}

such that for a generic value of
\lambda
the pair

(\widetilde{W}_{\lambda},\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}_{\lambda})
is of the type

(\widetilde{W},p)
. We allow for the curve
\widetilde{W}_{\lambda}
, as an algebraic
curve of
\left({{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}\right)^{2}
, some degeneracies of the two following types.

—

The two
(1,1)
curves of
\widetilde{W}_{\lambda}
degenerate into a double
(1,1)

curve. Then the projection
\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}_{\lambda}
becomes an isomorphism.

—

The two
(1,1)
curves of
\widetilde{W}_{\lambda}
degenerate into two double lines
intersecting at only one point
B
(
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
,
A^{+}
and
A^{-}
glue
together into the point
B
).

7
Geometry, surgery and pants

We will freely use some notations introduced in section

1.2
.

7.1
The classical geometry of a smooth cubic complex surface and
the representations of a free group of rank
3

There are strong relations between the classical geometry of a smooth complex
cubic surface (
27
lines,
45
tritangent planes …,
cf.

[
14
]
) and some
properties of the representations into
{\text{SL}}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})
of a free group of
rank
3
. As far as we know these (simple but important) relations remained unnoticed
until recently
50
50

50

They are due to Martin Klimes, Emmanuel Paul and the second
author, and they are studied in a work in progress on the confluence of the Painlevé
equations
[
42
,
43
]
.
.

7.1.1
The geometry of the cubic surfaces
\mathcal{S}(a)

By the classical theory, if
\Sigma
is a smooth projective cubic surface:

—

\Sigma
admits
27
lines and each line is a
(-1)
-line;

—

\Sigma
admits
45
tritangent planes;

—

the intersection of each tri-tangent plane with
\Sigma
is the union of
3
lines
forming a triangle;

—

each line of
\Sigma
belongs exactly to
5
tri-tangent planes.

The equation of the projective surface

\overline{\mathcal{S}(a)}\subset\mathbf{P}^{3}({\mathbf{C}})
in projective coordinates

(\tilde{X}_{0},\tilde{X}_{t},\tilde{X}_{1},\tilde{T})
is :

\tilde{X}_{0}\tilde{X}_{t}\tilde{X}_{1}+\tilde{X}_{0}^{2}\tilde{T}+\tilde{X}_{t}^{2}\tilde{T}+\tilde{X}_{1}^{2}\tilde{T}-A_{0}\tilde{X}_{0}\tilde{T}^{2}-A_{t}\tilde{X}_{t}\tilde{T}^{2}-A_{1}\tilde{X}_{1}\tilde{T}^{2}+A_{\infty}\tilde{T}^{3}=0.

The plane at infinity
\tilde{T}=0
is a tri-tangent plane and its intersection
with the surface is the triangle
\tilde{X}_{0}\tilde{X}_{t}\tilde{X}_{1}=0
. Therefore
the affine cubic surface
\mathcal{S}(a)
contains exactly
24
lines. We have
the following description of these lines.

Each line at infinity is contained in
4
tri-tangent planes different from
the plane at infinity. The intersection of such a tri-tangent plane and

\overline{\mathcal{S}(a)}
is a triangle, therefore the intersection with

\mathcal{S}(a)
is the union of
2
affine lines with a common point.
Therefore for each line at infinity we get
8
affine lines on
\mathcal{S}(a)
.
Using the coordinates
X_{0},X_{t},X_{1}
we see that for each
l=0,t,1

there exists
4
exceptional values of
X_{l}
such that

\{X_{l}=0\}\cap\mathcal{S}(a)
is the union of
2
affine lines.

Below we will interpret the
24
lines on
\mathcal{S}(a)
in terms of representations.

Proposition 7.1

Let
a_{0},a_{t},a_{1},a_{\infty}\in{\mathbf{C}}

arbitrary
. The
24
lines
distinct
or not
defined in
{\mathbf{C}}^{3}
by the following equations are contained in the cubic
surface
\mathcal{S}(a)\subset{\mathbf{C}}^{3}
:

(7.1.1)

X_{k}=e_{i}e_{j}^{-1}+e_{j}e_{i}^{-1},\quad e_{i}X_{i}+e_{j}X_{j}=a_{\infty}+e_{i}e_{j}a_{k},

X_{k}=e_{i}e_{j}^{-1}+e_{j}e_{i}^{-1},\quad e_{i}X_{j}+e_{j}X_{i}=a_{k}+e_{i}e_{j}a_{\infty},

X_{k}=e_{i}e_{j}+e_{i}^{-1}e_{j}^{-1},\quad X_{i}+e_{i}e_{j}X_{j}=e_{j}a_{k}+e_{i}a_{\infty},

X_{k}=e_{i}e_{j}+e_{i}^{-1}e_{j}^{-1},\quad X_{j}+e_{i}e_{j}X_{i}=e_{j}a_{\infty}+e_{i}a_{k},

X_{k}=e_{k}e_{\infty}^{-1}+e_{\infty}e_{k}^{-1},\quad e_{\infty}X_{i}+e_{k}X_{j}=a_{i}+e_{k}e_{\infty}a_{j},

X_{k}=e_{k}e_{\infty}^{-1}+e_{\infty}e_{k}^{-1},\quad e_{k}X_{i}+e_{\infty}X_{j}=a_{j}+e_{k}e_{\infty}a_{i},

X_{k}=e_{k}e_{\infty}+e_{k}^{-1}e_{\infty}^{-1},\quad X_{i}+e_{k}e_{\infty}X_{j}=e_{k}a_{j}+e_{\infty}a_{i},

X_{k}=e_{k}e_{\infty}+e_{k}^{-1}e_{\infty}^{-1},\quad X_{j}+e_{k}e_{\infty}X_{i}=e_{k}a_{i}+e_{\infty}a_{j}.

Proof. -
The result follows immediately from some decompositions of
F(X,a)
(
cf.

[
42
]
,
proposition 4.5). We give only one of these decompositions :

\displaystyle F(X:a)

\displaystyle=(X_{k}-e_{i}e_{j}^{-1}-e_{j}e_{i}^{-1})(F_{X_{k}}-X_{k}+e_{i}e_{j}^{-1}+e_{j}e_{i}^{-1})

\displaystyle-e_{i}^{-1}e_{j}^{-1}(e_{i}X_{i}+e_{j}X_{j}-a_{\infty}-e_{i}e_{j}a_{k})(e_{i}X_{j}+e_{j}X_{i}-a_{k}-e_{i}e_{j}a_{\infty}).

\Box

7.1.2
Reducibility of representations of the free group of rank
2

We recall the well known conditions of reducibility for the representations
of a free group of rank
2
into
{\text{SL}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})}
and some classical
results (
cf.

[
33
]
). We denote
\Gamma_{2}:=\langle u,v\rangle
the free group of
rank
2
generated by the letters
u,\penalty\ v
.

Definition 7.2

A pair of matrices
(M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime})\in\left({\text{SL}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})}\right)^{2}
is said

reducible
if there exists a common (non trivial, non total) invariant
subspace.

It is equivalent to say that the corresponding representation

\omega:\Gamma_{2}\rightarrow{\text{SL}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})}
defined by

\omega(u):=M^{\prime}
and
\omega(v):=M^{\prime\prime}
is reducible.

Let
\omega:\penalty\ \Gamma_{2}\rightarrow{\text{SL}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})}
be a linear representation.
We set
M^{\prime}:=\omega(u)
and
M^{\prime\prime}:=\omega(v)
. We denote
e^{\prime}
and
(e^{\prime})^{-1}

(resp.
e^{\prime\prime}
and
(e^{\prime\prime})^{-1}
) the eigenvalues of
M^{\prime}
(resp.
M^{\prime\prime}
). We denote

e
and
e^{-1}
the eigenvalues of
M:=M^{\prime}M^{\prime\prime}
.

Proposition 7.3

The following assertions are equivalent

(i)

The representation
\omega
is reducible.

(ii)

The pair
(M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime})
is reducible.

(iii)

We have :
e=e^{\prime}e^{\prime\prime}
or
e=e^{\prime}(e^{\prime\prime})^{-1}
or
e=(e^{\prime})^{-1}e^{\prime\prime}
or

e=(e^{\prime})^{-1}(e^{\prime\prime})^{-1}
.

(iv)

We have
{\text{Tr}}\penalty\ M=e^{\prime}e^{\prime\prime}+(e^{\prime}e^{\prime\prime})^{-1}
or

{\text{Tr}}\penalty\ M=e^{\prime}(e^{\prime\prime})^{-1}+(e^{\prime})^{-1}e^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof. -

—

The assertions (i) and (ii) are evidently equivalent.

—

If the pair

(M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime})
is reducible, then there exists a common eigenvector
v\in{\mathbf{C}}^{2}
.
Then
M^{\prime}v=\lambda^{\prime}v
,
M^{\prime\prime}v=\lambda^{\prime\prime}v
and
Mv=\lambda v
, where
\lambda^{\prime}
,

\lambda^{\prime\prime}
and
\lambda
are respectively eigenvalues of
M^{\prime}
,
M^{\prime\prime}
and
M
.
Therefore one of the conditions of (iii) is satisfied.

—

The assertion (iii) implies clearly the assertion (iv).

—

If
\omega
is irreducible, then one can prove that :

{\text{Tr}}M\neq e^{\prime}e^{\prime\prime}+(e^{\prime}e^{\prime\prime})^{-1}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{\emph{and}}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\text{Tr}}\penalty\ M\neq e^{\prime}(e^{\prime\prime})^{-1}+(e^{\prime})^{-1}e^{\prime\prime}

(
cf.

[
33
]
, (4.2.9), page 83). Therefore if (iv) is satisfied, then
\omega

is necessarily reducible. This proves the assertion (i).

\Box

Let
\omega
be a representation and
M^{\prime}
,
M^{\prime\prime}
as above. We suppose that

{\text{Tr}}\penalty\ M^{\prime}\neq\pm 2
and
{\text{Tr}}\penalty\ M^{\prime\prime}\neq\pm 2
. Then
M^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime\prime}
are diagonalisable.
There allways exists a
mixed basis

\{v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\}
of
{\mathbf{C}}^{2}
formed by an eigenvector
of
M^{\prime}
and an eigenvector of
M^{\prime\prime}
. In general there are (up to rescaling of the
eigenvectors)
4
ways one can form such a basis. The
4
cases of
reducibility of
\omega
correspond to the degeneracy of one of these
4
basis.

We recall that if
\omega
is irreducible, then it is determined, up to equivalence,
by the traces of
M^{\prime}
,
M^{\prime\prime}
and
M
(
cf.

[
33
]
Theorem 4.2.1, page 80).

7.1.3
Partial reducibility and lines on
\mathcal{S}(a)

We describe a relation between a notion of partial reducibility of a representation
and the lines on the cubic surface
\mathcal{S}(a)
. This relation is apparently
new
51
51

51

If we replace representations by wild representations, then this relation
can be extended to all the Painlevé equations.
, it has been found recently by
M. Klimes, E. Paul and the second author
[
42
,
43
]
.

As in
1.2.1
, we denote
\Gamma_{3}:=\langle u_{0},u_{t},u_{1}\rangle
the free group
of rank
3

generated by the letters
u_{0},\penalty\ u_{t},\penalty\ u_{1}
and we set
u_{\infty}=u_{1}^{-1}u_{t}^{-1}u_{0}^{-1}
.
Let
\rho:\Gamma_{3}\rightarrow{\text{SL}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})}
be a linear representation.
We set
M_{l}:=\rho(u_{i})
(
i=0,t,1,\infty
). We denote

e_{l}
and
e_{l}^{-1}
(
i=0,t,1,\infty
) the eigenvalues of
M_{i}
.

We have the following characterizations of smoothness (some are classical and some
are apparently new).

Theorem 7.4

Let
a\in{\mathbf{C}}^{4}
. We suppose
a_{l}\neq\pm 2
(
l=0,t,1,\infty
) (non resonance).
The following conditions are equivalent :

(i)

The affine cubic surface
\mathcal{S}(a)
is smooth.

(ii)

The projective cubic surface
\overline{\mathcal{S}(a)}
is smooth.

(iii)

The
24
lines (
7.1.1
) are pairwise distinct.

(iv)

The
3
following conditions are satisfied:

—

the
4
numbers built from the
e_{l}
(
l=0,t,1,\infty
)

(7.4.1)

e_{t}e_{1}^{-1}+e_{1}e_{t}^{-1},\penalty\ \penalty\ e_{t}e_{1}+e_{t}^{-1}e_{1}^{-1},\penalty\ \penalty\ e_{0}e_{\infty}^{-1}+e_{\infty}e_{0}^{-1},\penalty\ \penalty\ e_{0}e_{\infty}+e_{0}^{-1}e_{\infty}^{-1}

are pairwise distinct;

—

the
4
numbers :

(7.4.2)

e_{1}e_{\infty}^{-1}+e_{\infty}e_{1}^{-1},\penalty\ \penalty\ e_{1}e_{\infty}+e_{1}^{-1}e_{\infty}^{-1},\penalty\ \penalty\ e_{t}e_{0}^{-1}+e_{0}e_{t}^{-1},\penalty\ \penalty\ e_{t}e_{0}+e_{t}^{-1}e_{0}^{-1}

are pairwise distinct,

—

the
4
numbers :

(7.4.3)

e_{1}e_{\infty}^{-1}+e_{\infty}e_{1}^{-1},\penalty\ \penalty\ e_{1}e_{\infty}+e_{1}^{-1}e_{\infty}^{-1},\penalty\ \penalty\ e_{t}e_{0}^{-1}+e_{0}e_{t}^{-1},\penalty\ \penalty\ e_{t}e_{0}+e_{t}^{-1}e_{0}^{-1}

are pairwise distinct.

(v)

We have the
8
conditions:
e_{0}\,e_{t}^{\pm 1}e_{1}^{\pm 1}e_{\infty}^{\pm 1}\neq 1

(the
3
signs are chosen independantly).

(vi)

If
\rho
is a representation such that
{\text{Tr}}\penalty\ \rho(u_{l})=a_{l}
for all
l=0,t,1,\infty
,
then it is irreducible.

Proof. -

—

A singular point of
\overline{\mathcal{S}(a)}
is allways contained into
\mathcal{S}(a)
,
therefore (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii).

—

The conditions (iv) and (v) are clearly equivalent.

—

We have (iii)
\Leftrightarrow
(i).
If the
24
lines are distinct, then
\overline{\mathcal{S}(a)}
contains
27
distinct
lines and therefore it is smooth.

—

We have (iv)
\Rightarrow
(iii). If we suppose (iv), then we get
3
sets of
8
two
by two distinct lines. Each set corresponds to
4
distinct values of
X_{0}
or
X_{t}

or
X_{1}
. It is easy to check that a line cannot belong to
2
different such sets.

—

We have (i)
\Rightarrow
(v)
\Rightarrow
(iii).
If
\mathcal{S}(a)
is smooth, then (
1.0.3
) is impossible, therefore (v)
is true; (iv) and (iii) follows.

—

We have (ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(vi). Easy.

\Box

If we use the parameters
\theta_{l}
, then the conditions (v) are translated into:

\theta_{0}\pm\theta_{l}\pm\theta_{1}\pm\theta_{\infty}\in{\mathbf{Z}}

(
cf.

[
47
]
Theorem 4.1).

We suppose that the surface
\mathcal{S}(a)
is smooth.
For each pair
(l,m)
of elements of
\{0,t,1,\infty\}
, each of the
2
planes :

(7.4.4)

\begin{cases}X_{n}=e_{l}e_{m}+e_{l}^{-1}e_{m}^{-1}\\
X_{n}=e_{l}e_{m}^{-1}+e_{l}^{-1}e_{m}\end{cases}\text{\penalty\ \penalty\ with}\penalty\ \penalty\ (l,m,n)=\begin{cases}(i,j,k)\\
(k,\infty,k)\end{cases}

intersects
\mathcal{S}(a)
at
2
lines. The resulting
4
lines correspond to
the reducibility of the pair of matrices
(M_{l},M_{m})
.

More precisely if two matrices
M_{l}
and
M_{m}
are diagonalizable, for each of them
there exists a pair of invariant subspaces giving rise to a basis. Then there are
in general
4
possibilities of pairing of invariant subspaces out of which one can
form a
mixed
basis. The cases of reducibility of the pair
(M_{l},M_{m})
corresponds
to the degeneracy of (at least) one of these mixed bases. Each of the
4
lines
corresponds to such a case of degeneracy.

Definition 7.5

Let
\rho:\Gamma_{3}\rightarrow{\text{SL}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})}
. We will say that
\rho
is

partially reducible
if there exists
i,j\in\{0,t,1,\infty\}
,
i\neq j
,
such that the pair of matrices
\left(\rho(u_{i}),\rho(u_{j})\right)
is reducible.

Proposition 7.6

Let
a\in{\mathbf{C}}^{4}
arbitrary and
\rho:\Gamma_{3}\rightarrow\text{SL}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})
a representation
such that
{\text{Tr}}\,\rho(u_{l})=a_{l}
(
l=0,t,1,\infty
).

(i)

If the representation
\rho
is
partially reducible
, then
its equivalence class belongs to one of the
24
lines (distinct or not) defined in
proposition
7.1
.

(ii)

We suppose that
\mathcal{S}(a)
is smooth. Then
\rho
is partially reducible if
and only if its equivalence class belongs to one of the
24
lines of

\mathcal{S}(a)
.

Proof. -
The representation
\rho
is partially reducible if and only if there exists
k=1,2,3

such that :

(7.6.1)

X_{k}=e_{i}e_{j}^{-1}+e_{i}e_{j}^{-1}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{or}\penalty\ \penalty\ X_{k}=e_{i}e_{j}+e_{i}^{-1}e_{j}^{-1}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{or}\penalty\ \penalty\ X_{k}=e_{k}e_{\infty}^{-1}+e_{\infty}e_{k}^{-1}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{or}\penalty\ \penalty\ X_{k}=e_{k}e_{\infty}+e_{k}^{-1}e_{\infty}^{-1}.

Then assertion (i) follows from proposition
7.1
and assertion (ii)
follows from theorem
7.4
.

\Box

The condition (
7.6.1
) appear in various papers in “
theta
notation”:

\sigma_{k}\pm\theta_{i}\pm\theta_{j}\in 2{\mathbf{Z}},\quad\sigma_{k}\pm\theta_{k}\pm\theta_{\infty}\in 2{\mathbf{Z}},

where
X_{k}=e^{i\pi\sigma_{k}}+e^{-i\pi\sigma_{k}}
.

cf.

[
37
]
p 1141: condition
(A.3)_{PVI}
and

[

]
, footnote
9
, page

.

A fibration.

We suppose that we are in the ”generic case” (i. e.
{\mathcal{S}}_{VI}(a)
is smooth).

Let
\Pi_{0}:{\mathcal{S}}_{VI}(a)\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
,
\Pi_{0}:(X_{0},X_{t},X_{\infty})\mapsto X_{0}
.
We recall :

{\mathcal{S}}_{VI}(a)=\left\{(X_{0},X_{t},X_{1})\in{\mathbf{C}}^{3}\mid X_{0}X_{t}X_{1}+X_{0}^{2}+X_{t}^{2}+X_{1}^{2}-A_{0}X_{0}-A_{t}X_{t}-A_{1}X_{1}+A_{\infty}=0\right\}.

For
c\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
\Pi_{0}^{-1}(c)
is interpreted as an affine conic in the

(X_{t},X_{1})
-plane:

X_{t}^{2}+X_{1}^{2}+cX_{t}X_{1}-A_{t}X_{t}-A_{1}X_{1}-cA_{0}+A_{\infty}=0.

The generic fiber is isomorphic to
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
. The exceptional fibers are of of two types :

—

either
X_{0}=\pm 2
, then

X_{t}^{2}+X_{1}^{2}\pm 2X_{t}X_{1}=(X_{t}\pm X_{1})^{2}
,
the fiber is a
parabola
and it is isomorphic to
{\mathbf{C}}
;

—

either we are in a partially reductible case, that is in one of the
4
cases :

(7.6.2)

X_{0}=e_{t}e_{1}^{-1}+e_{1}e_{t}^{-1}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{or}\penalty\ \penalty\ X_{0}=e_{t}e_{1}+e_{t}^{-1}e_{1}^{-1}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{or}\penalty\ \penalty\ X_{0}=e_{0}e_{\infty}^{-1}+e_{\infty}e_{0}^{-1}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{or}\penalty\ \penalty\ X_{0}=e_{0}e_{\infty}+e_{0}^{-1}e_{\infty}^{-1},

then the fiber is
degenerated into two lines
. The intersection of these
two lines is a critical point of
\Pi_{0}
. Its image is a critical value of
\Pi_{0}
.

If we remove the
6
exceptional fibers (that is
8
lines and two curves)
from
{\mathcal{S}}_{VI}(a)
then we can parameterize the remaining set by
a Zariski open set of
{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
. Such parameterizations appear in
many papers
[
37
]
,
[
31
]
…We will return to this
question below (
cf.

7.2.2
).
In the
q
-case, we will deduce later from the Mano decomposition a similar
parameterization (
cf.

7.2.3
).

If
c_{0}
is a critical value of
\Pi_{0}
, then we can describe

\Pi_{0}^{-1}(\Delta)
for a small open disc
\Delta\subset{\mathbf{C}}
centered at
c_{0}
.
We blow down one of the lines of

\Pi^{-1}(c_{0})
into the smooth surface
\Pi_{0}^{-1}(\Delta)

(the closure of such a line in
\overline{\mathcal{S}(a)}
is a
(-1)
-line).
We get a surface analytically diffeomorphic to

\Delta\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
. Therefore
\Pi_{0}^{-1}(\Delta)
is
analytically diffeomorphic to a blow-up of
\Delta\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
.

The projective version of the above description is the following.
We choose one of the lines
\Delta
of the triangle at infinity of
the surface
\overline{\mathcal{S}_{VI}(a)}
. The family of planes passing
by this line cut the surface along a linear family of cubic curves.
Each one is decomposed into the union of
\Delta
and a conic curve.
There are
5
conic curves degenerated into
2
lines:
they correspond to the
5
tritangent planes, the plane at infinity
and
4
other planes. There are two other exceptional curves: when
the conic is tangent to
\Delta
.

A simple model.

We will give two descriptions of a hyperbolic paraboloid of
{\mathbf{C}}^{3}

(an affine quadric surface) :

—

by a blow-up of a point in
{\mathbf{C}}^{2}
;

—

by a singular fibration.

Let
\mathcal{Q}\subset{\mathbf{C}}^{3}
defined by the equation
Y=XZ
(hyperbolic
paraboloid). Let
f:{\mathbf{C}}^{3}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
defined by
f:(X,Y,Z)\mapsto Y

and
g:{\mathbf{C}}^{3}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}^{2}
(the
(X,Y)
plane) defined by

g:(X,Y,Z)\mapsto\left(X,f(X,Y,Z)=XZ\right)
. We denote

\Phi
(resp.
\phi
) the restriction of
f
(resp.
g
) to

\mathcal{Q}
.

—

The map
\phi
is a bijection of

\mathcal{Q}\setminus\phi^{-1}(0,0)
onto
{\mathbf{C}}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}

and
\phi^{-1}(0,0)
is the line of
\mathcal{Q}
defined by

\{(X,Y,Z)\in C^{3}|\penalty\ X=Y=0\}
. If we parameterize
\mathcal{Q}

by
(X,Z)
then
\phi
is expressed by
(X,T)\mapsto(X,Y=XT)
.
The quadric
\mathcal{Q}
is a blow-up of
{\mathbf{C}}^{2}
at the point
(0,0)
.
If we blow down into
\mathcal{Q}
the line
(X=Z=0)
by
g
we get
{\mathbf{C}}^{2}
.

—

We can describle
\mathcal{Q}
by the fibers
\Phi^{-1}(c)
of
\Phi
.
If
c\neq 0
, then the fiber is the affine conic

\{(X,c,Z)\in{\mathbf{C}}^{3}|\penalty\ X\neq 0,\penalty\ Z=c/X\}
. If
c=0
, then the fiber is
the union of the two lines
(X=0)
and
(Z=0)
.

7.1.4
Dynamics on
{\mathcal{S}}_{VI}(a)

The cubic surface of
P_{VI}
admits
3
polynomial involutions
s_{0},\penalty\ s_{t},\penalty\ s_{1}
.
These involutions are
anti-symplectic
and they generate a subgroup of
the group of algebraic automorphisms of
\mathcal{S}_{VI}
which is isomorphic
to
{\mathbf{Z}}_{2}*{\mathbf{Z}}_{2}*{\mathbf{Z}}_{2}

[
12
, Theorem 3.1, page 2948]
. The products

g_{i,j}:=s_{i}\circ s_{j}
(
i\neq j
) are
symplectic polynomial automorphisms
.
The corresponding automorphisms of the Okamoto variety of initial conditions
obtained by conjugation by
RH
are the
non-linear monodromies
of PVI
around the singular points
0,1,\infty

[
12
]
.

7.2
Pant decompositions, surgery, parametrizations

7.2.1
Pant decompositions of representations of a free group of rank
3
.

Let
\rho:\Gamma_{3}\rightarrow{\text{SL}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})}
be a linear representation. We use the same
notations as before. We suppose
a
fixed such that
a_{l}={\text{Tr}}\penalty\ M_{l}\neq\pm 2
,

l=0,t,1,\infty
. We associate to
\rho
the two representations of
\Gamma_{2}
:

\omega_{0,t}:\Gamma_{2}\rightarrow\text{SL}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \omega_{1,\infty}:\Gamma_{2}\rightarrow\text{SL}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})

We will say that
(\omega_{0,t},\omega_{1,\infty})
is the
pant decomposition

of
\rho
associated to the partition
\{0,t,1,\infty\}=\{0,t\}\cup\{1,\infty\}
.
There are two others pant decompositions associated to the two other partitions.

We denote
\tilde{\rho}
and
\tilde{\omega}
the equivalence classes of representations.
The pair
(\tilde{\omega}_{0,t},\tilde{\omega}_{1,\infty})
depends only on
\tilde{\rho}
.

We suppose that
\omega_{0,t}
and
\omega_{1,\infty}
are irreducible, equivalently

\tilde{\rho}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(a)
minus the
8
critical lines. Then the
knowledge of
(\tilde{\omega}_{0,t},\tilde{\omega}_{1,\infty})
is equivalent to the
knowledge of
X_{0}={\text{Tr}}\penalty\ M_{0}M_{t}={\text{Tr}}\penalty\ M_{1}M_{\infty}
(
a
is fixed).

In order to recover
\tilde{\rho}
from
X_{0}
, we need another parameter.
We set
X_{0}=e+e^{-1}
. There are two different cases.

—

We suppose
X_{0}\neq\pm 2
(equivalently
e\neq e^{-1}
).
We can choose representations
\omega^{\prime}
and
\omega^{\prime\prime}
of
\Gamma_{2}
such that :

{\text{Tr}}\penalty\ \omega^{\prime}(u)=a_{0},\penalty\ \penalty\ {\text{Tr}}\penalty\ \omega^{\prime}(v)=a_{t},\penalty\ \penalty\ \omega^{\prime}(uv)={\text{Diag}}(e,e^{-1}),

{\text{Tr}}\penalty\ \omega^{\prime\prime}(u)=a_{\infty},\penalty\ \penalty\ {\text{Tr}}\penalty\ \omega^{\prime\prime}(v)=a_{1},\penalty\ \penalty\ \omega^{\prime\prime}(uv)={\text{Diag}}(e^{-1},e).

There is a freeness in the choice: we can replace
\omega^{\prime}
(resp.
\omega^{\prime\prime}
)
by an overall conjugate by an arbitrary matrix commuting with
{\text{Diag}}(e,e^{-1})
,
that is of the form
{\text{Diag}}(t,t^{-1})
with
t\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}

—

We suppose
X_{0}=\pm 2
. We verify that the “trivial case”
\omega^{\prime}(uv)=\pm I_{2}

is impossible. Then we can choose representations
\omega^{\prime}
and
\omega^{\prime\prime}
of

\Gamma_{2}
such that :

{\text{Tr}}\penalty\ \omega^{\prime}(u)=a_{0},\penalty\ \penalty\ {\text{Tr}}\penalty\ \omega^{\prime}(v)=a_{t},\penalty\ \penalty\ \omega^{\prime}(uv)=\begin{pmatrix}e&1\\
0&e\end{pmatrix},

{\text{Tr}}\penalty\ \omega^{\prime\prime}(u)=a_{\infty},\penalty\ \penalty\ {\text{Tr}}\penalty\ \omega^{\prime\prime}(v)=a_{1},\penalty\ \penalty\ \omega^{\prime\prime}(uv)=\begin{pmatrix}e^{-1}&-1\\
0&e^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

There is a freeness in the choice: we can replace
\omega^{\prime}
(resp.
\omega^{\prime\prime}
) by
an overall conjugate by an arbitrary matrix commuting with

\begin{pmatrix}e&1\\
0&e\end{pmatrix}
, that is of the form

\begin{pmatrix}1&t\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
with
t\in{\mathbf{C}}
.

7.2.2
Pant decompositions and pant parametrizations

Pant decompositions of a
n
-punctured sphere. Trace coordinates.

Let
n\in{\mathbf{N}}
,
n\geq 4
. We denote
S_{n}^{2}
the
n
-punctured sphere. We replace
the
n
punctures by little holes obtained by cutting along non-intersecting simple
closed curves surrounding the punctures, we get a
n
-holed sphere
that we
also denote
S_{n}^{2}
.

Definition 7.7

A
pant decomposition
is defined by cutting
S_{n}^{2}
along
n-3
simple closed
curves
\gamma_{r}
,
r=1,\ldots,n-3
, on
S_{n}^{2}
in such a way that this will decompose

S_{n}^{2}
into a disjoint union of
n-2
three-holed spheres
S_{3,t}^{2}
,
t=1,\ldots,n-2
.
The collection
\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n-3}\}
of curves is called the
cut
system
.

The origin of the terminology is clear: a pair of pants is homeomorphic to a
3

holed sphere.

If
n=4
, then the cut system if a set of
3
curves. Each curve separates the set
of punctures
\{0,t,1,\infty\}
into two unordered pairs of unordered sets of two
elements:

\left((0,t),(1,\infty)\right),\penalty\ \penalty\ \left((0,1),(t,\infty)\right),\penalty\ \penalty\ \left((0,\infty),(t,1)\right).

We will also call (abusively …) such a pair
a pant decomposition
of
the
4
-punctured sphere. In that sense we get
3
pant decompositions.

We can interpret the character variety
\mathcal{S}
associated to the free
group
\Gamma_{n-1}
(of rank
n-1
) as the set of representations
\rho
of

\pi_{1}(S_{n}^{2})
into
{\text{SL}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})}
modulo equivalence of representations.
Then useful sets of coordinates on
\mathcal{S}
are given by the trace functions

{\text{Tr}}\,\rho(\gamma)
associated to any simple closed curve
\gamma
on
S_{4}^{2}
.
It is a classical fact that minimal sets of trace functions that can be used to
parameterize
\mathcal{S}
can be identified using
pant decompositions
.
We will give only the basic idea. For more details see for example
[
31
]
(that
we follow in our description). In the next part we will detail the case
n=4
. To a
pant decomposition we can associate decompositions of a representation
\rho
. More
abstractly we get a notion of pant decomposition of a representation of a free group
of rank
n
. This generalizes the definition introduced in the preceding paragraph.

To each curve
\gamma_{r}
we can associate the union of the two
3
-holed spheres
which have
\gamma_{r}
in their boundary (one of the
3
components). We get a
4
-holed
sphere
S_{n,r}^{2}
. We choose an orientation on each curve
\gamma_{r}
. This allows us
to introduce a numbering of the
4
boundary components of
S_{n,r}^{2}
. Then we can
consider the curves
\gamma_{s}^{r}
and
\gamma_{t}^{r}
which encircle respectively the
pair of component
(1,2)
and
(2,3)
. The collection of pairs of trace functions

\left({\text{Tr}}\,\rho(\gamma_{s}^{r}),{\text{Tr}}\,\rho(\gamma_{t}^{r})\right)
,
r=1,\ldots n-3
, can
be used to parameterize the character variety.

Pant parametrization of a
4
holed sphere. Jimbo formulae.

Let
S_{4}^{2}
be the four punctured sphere. Its fundamental group
\pi_{1}(S_{4}^{2})
is
isomorphic to a free group of rank
3
: we can choose as generators the homotopy
classes of three simple loops turning around
3
punctures. We choose simple loops

\gamma_{i}
,
i=1,2,3,4
turning respectively around the
4
punctures and based at
a point

of the punctured sphere.

As above, up to a Möbius transformation, we can choose as punctures
0,t,1,\infty

for some value of
t\in{\mathbf{C}}\setminus\{0,1\}
. Then we denote
\gamma_{i}
,
i=0,t,1,\infty
,
the simple loops and
M_{i}=\rho(\gamma_{i})

M_{i}
semi-simple with
eigenvalues
e_{i},e_{i}^{-1}
and we set, as above,

a_{i}:={\text{Tr}}\,M_{i}=e_{i}+e_{i}^{-1}
,
i=0,t,1,\infty
, and

X_{0}={\text{Tr}}\penalty\ M_{1}M_{t},\quad X_{t}={\text{Tr}}\penalty\ M_{1}M_{0},\quad X_{1}={\text{Tr}}\penalty\ M_{t}M_{0}.

We can apply this to the monodromy representation of a system
1.1.3
, then
we have local monodromy exponents

and
a_{i}=2\cos\theta_{i}
. We suppose
that the non resonance conditions are satisfied:
a_{i}\neq\pm 2
or equivalently

\theta_{i}\notin{\mathbf{Z}}
.

We can interpret
M_{t}M_{0}
as the monodromy associated to an oriented curve separating
the singularities in two packs
(0,t)
and
(1,\infty)

punctured

into two

More precisely the above formulae give a rational parametrization of the conic if

Lemma 7.8

We suppose the
\rho_{i}
and
\sigma_{j}
(
i,j=1,2
) fixed (satisfying the “good
conditions”). We have :

\theta_{q}\left(\frac{\xi_{j}}{\rho_{i}}x\right)\in V_{1,\frac{\rho_{i}}{\xi_{j}}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \theta_{q}\left(\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\xi_{i}}x\right)\in V_{1,\frac{\xi_{i}}{\sigma_{j}}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \theta_{q}\left(\frac{\xi_{h}}{\rho_{i}}x\right)\theta_{q}\left(\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\xi_{h}}x\right)\in V_{2,\frac{\rho_{i}}{\sigma_{j}}}.

The maps
\zeta_{i,j,h}:{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\mapsto V_{2,\frac{\rho_{i}}{\sigma_{j}}}
(
i,j,h=1,2
) defined by

\zeta_{i,j,h}:\xi_{h}\mapsto\theta_{q}\left(\frac{\xi_{h}}{\rho_{i}}x\right)\theta_{q}\left(\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\xi_{h}}x\right)

are analytic on
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
.

Proof. -
We fix
(i,j)
. Let
(e_{1},e_{2})
be a basis of
V_{2,\frac{\rho_{i}}{\sigma_{j}}}
. There exist
two functions
C_{1}(\xi_{h})
and
C_{2}(\xi_{h})
of
\xi_{h}
, uniquely determined, such that :

\zeta_{i,j,h}(\xi_{h})=C_{1}(\xi_{h})e_{1}+C_{1}(\xi_{h})e_{2}.

We have :

\zeta_{i,j,h}(\xi_{h})(qx)=C_{1}(\xi_{h})e_{1}(qx)+C_{1}(\xi_{h})e_{2}(qx).

For
x\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
fixed, the two functions :

\xi_{h}\rightarrow\zeta_{i,j,h}(\xi_{h})(x)
and

\xi_{h}\rightarrow\zeta_{i,j,h}(\xi_{h})(qx)
are analytic on
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
, therefore
C_{1}

and
C_{2}
are analytic on
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
.

\Box

The functions
s
and
t
are meromorphic on
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
, they are analytic on the inverse
image
U^{\bullet,{\dagger};1,2}
of
{\mathbf{E}_{q}}^{\bullet,{\dagger};1,2}
. Identifying

with a set of matrices, we define a map

by :

(7.8.1)

\underline{M}_{1,2}(\xi,\eta):=P_{1,2}(\xi,x)\,{\text{Diag}}(1,\eta)\,Q_{1,2}(\xi,x).

If we fix
\xi

is
linear
in

The map

is analytic in the variable

. We have :

. Therefore

Remark 7.9

In

It is possible to compute explicitely a system of algebraic charts for

Then the surface

A smoothness conjecture.

. This conjecture is strongly related to the configuration of the

{\mathcal{F}}
. We will return to this question in the next paragraphs.

We set
\mathcal{U}_{i,j}={\mathcal{F}}\setminus\left(\Pi_{i,j}^{-1}(0)\cup\Pi_{i,j}^{-1}(\infty)\cup(\Pi^{\prime})_{i,j}^{-1}(0)\cup(\Pi^{\prime})_{i,j}^{-1}(\infty)\right)
; it is
{\mathcal{F}}
minus the
4
exceptional non logarithmic
fibers. The image of
\psi_{i,j}
is
{\mathcal{F}}
minus all the exceptional fibers, therefore
it is
\mathcal{U}_{i,j}
minus the logarithmic fibers.

Conjecture 7.10

We suppose that (FR), (NR), (NS) and

\mathbf{Hyp}_{48}

{\mathcal{F}}
is
smooth
.

57

8

of the six

). Then the above

6.8

The rational functions
\Pi_{i,j}
on the surface
{\mathcal{F}}
can be interpreted as
q

of the Fricke coordinates. Then there are natural questions:

—

What are the algebraic relations between the
\Pi_{i,j}

—

Is it possible to use the
\Pi_{i,j}

(

7.2.4
q
-pants decompositions and partial reducibility

We consider the quotient of
{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})
by the action of
q^{\mathbf{Z}}
. We write it :

\left[{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}});q\right]:=\{[0;q]\}\cup{\mathbf{E}_{q}}\cup\{[\infty;q]\}.

We have a “cotangent bundle” (defined by the first projection) :

\left[{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}});q\right]\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\rightarrow\left[{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}});q\right].

It is the
q
-analog of the cotangent bundle of
{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})
(of more generally a
Riemann surface) in the differential case; we will call it the
q
-cotangent
bundle
.

The fiber above
[a;q]
is
[a;q]\times{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
. When the “point”
[a;q]
is a
singularity, we will consider
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
as the space of
possible monodromy
exponents
. More precisely above
[a;q]=[0;q]
or
[a;q]=[\infty;q]
we can choose
arbitrarily a monodromy exponent into
{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
but if
[a;q]\in{\mathbf{E}_{q}}
, the only possible
choice above
[a;q]
is
\zeta\in{\mathbf{C}}^{*}
such that
[\zeta:q]=[-a^{-1};q]
.

In the context of our description of
q
-PVI we consider the following list of pairs
of points of the total space of the
q
-cotangent bundle
58
58

58

We skip the problem
of ordering or not of such pair.
:

(7.10.1)

\left(\left([0;q];\rho_{1}\right),\left([0;q];\rho_{2}\right)\right),\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(\left([\infty;q];\sigma_{1}\right),\left([\infty;q];\sigma_{2}\right)\right),\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(\left([x_{i};q];-x_{i}^{-1}\right),\left([x_{j};q];-x_{j}^{-1}\right)\right).

(
i=1,2,3,4
,
i\neq j
). Be careful :

\left(\left([x_{i};q];-x_{i}^{-1}\right),\left([x_{j};q];-x_{j}^{-1}\right)\right)

is written in the chart of the
q
-cotangent bundle coming from
{\mathbf{C}}
. In the chart
coming from
{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})\setminus\{0\}
, we write the same element

\left(\left([x_{i}^{-1};q];-x_{i}\right),\left([x_{j}^{-1};q];-x_{j}\right)\right).

The Mano decomposition allows us to decompose the
global
monodromy
around
0
,
\infty
and the four intermediate singularities into a pair
of
local
monodromies : around
0
and one pair of singularities on
one side and one pair of singularities and
\infty
on the other side. As we
will see it is better to interpret
0
,
\infty
and the pair
(x_{i},x_{j})
as
the corresponding elements in the list (
7.10.1
).

We have a criterion of reducibility for each local monodromy. Using the above
list (
7.10.1
), we will see that it is a perfect
q
-analog of the
criterium in the differential case (
cf.
proposition
7.6
).

Looking on the left hand side of RH, the Mano decomposition can be interpreted as a
decomposion of the system into two hypergeometric systems. We recall that it is
the beginning of our story: in
[
45
]
Mano gave a
direct
method (based
on isomonodromy and a
q
-analogy with Jimbo decompoition
[
37
]
) in
order to decompose the original system.

The reducibility of a local monodromy on the right hand side of RH is equivalent to
the reducibility of the corresponding hypergeometric system on the left hand side (
cf.
subsection
3.3
).

The reducibility of the local monodromy around
0
and the pair
(x_{i},x_{j})
is coded by :

\left(\left([0;q];\rho_{1}\right),\left([0;q];\rho_{2}\right)\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(\left([x_{i};q];-x_{i}^{-1}\right),\left([x_{j};q];-x_{j}^{-1}\right)\right).

The
4
conditions of reducibility are :

\xi_{1}=-\rho_{1}/x_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \xi_{1}=-\rho_{1}/x_{j},\penalty\ \penalty\ \xi_{1}=-\rho_{2}/x_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \xi_{1}=-\rho_{2}/x_{j}.

They correspond to the following pairings :

\displaystyle\left([0;q];\rho_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\left([x_{i};q];-x_{i}^{-1}\right),\quad\left([0;q];\rho_{1}\right)\longleftrightarrow\left([x_{j};q];-x_{j}^{-1}\right),

\displaystyle\left([0;q];\rho_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\left([x_{i};q];-x_{i}^{-1}\right),\quad\left([0;q];\rho_{2}\right)\longleftrightarrow\left([x_{j};q];-x_{j}^{-1}\right).

For simplicity we will denote these pairings

(\rho_{h}\leftrightarrow x_{i})
(
h=1,2;\penalty\ i=1,2,3,4
).

The reducibility of the local monodromy around the pair

(x_{k},x_{l})
and
\infty
is coded by :

\left(\left([\infty;q];\sigma_{1}\right),\left([\infty;q];\sigma_{2}\right)\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(\left([x_{k}^{-1};q];-x_{k}\right),\left([x_{l}^{-1};q];-x_{l}\right)\right).

The
4
conditions of reducibility are :

\xi_{1}=-\sigma_{1}x_{k},\penalty\ \penalty\ \xi_{1}=-\sigma_{1}x_{l},\penalty\ \penalty\ \xi_{1}=-\sigma_{2}x_{k},\penalty\ \penalty\ \xi_{1}=-\sigma_{2}x_{l}.

They correspond to the following pairings :

\displaystyle\left([\infty;q];\sigma_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\left([x_{k}^{-1};q];-x_{k}\right),\quad\left([\infty;q];\sigma_{1}\right)\longleftrightarrow\left([x_{l}^{-1};q];-x_{l}\right),

\displaystyle\left([\infty;q];\sigma_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\left([x_{k}^{-1};q];-x_{k}\right),\quad\left([\infty;q];\sigma_{2}\right)\longleftrightarrow\left([x_{l}^{-1};q];-x_{l}\right).

For simplicity we will denote these pairings

(\sigma_{h}\leftrightarrow x_{i})
(
h=1,2;\penalty\ i=1,2,3,4
).

We will see in the next subsection that each pairing
\longleftrightarrow
corresponds
to a
line
on the surface
{\mathcal{F}}
. We have
8
pairings and therefore
8
lines.

The pairing
(\rho_{1}\leftrightarrow x_{i})
(resp.
(\rho_{2}\leftrightarrow x_{i})
)
appears in the
3
Mano decompositions
(i,j)
,
(i,k)
and
(i,l)
(where
(i,j,k,l)

is a permutation of
(1,2,3,4)
). In the next paragraph we will prove that the
corresponding
3
lines
coincide
.

Similarly the pairing
(\sigma_{1}\leftrightarrow x_{i^{\prime}})
(resp.

(\sigma_{2}\leftrightarrow x_{i^{\prime}})
) appears in the
3
Mano decompositions

(i,j)
,
(i,k)
and
(i,l)
(
(i,j,k,l)
(where
(i,j)=\left((i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\right)
…).
We will also prove that the corresponding
3
lines
coincide
.

7.2.5
Description of some lines on the surface
{\mathcal{F}}

In all this part we suppose that (FR), (NR), (NS) and
\mathbf{Hyp}_{48}
are satisfied.

We will give a global description of the special non-logarithmic lines on the surface

{\mathcal{F}}
and observe that this description is a translation by
q
-analogies of the
dictionnary between the set of lines on the cubic surface on one side and the partial
reducibility of representations of the other side that we described in the classical
cases (
cf.

7.1.3
).

We will derive from this description a notion of
reducibility of the local
monodromy around
0
and
x_{i}
(resp.
\infty
and
x_{i}
).

We recall :

\displaystyle\Phi_{1,2}\left(-\rho_{1}/x_{1}\right)=\Phi_{1,2}\left(-\rho_{2}/x_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\Phi_{1,2}\left(-\rho_{1}/x_{2}\right)=\Phi_{1,2}\left(-\rho_{2}/x_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\infty,

and we set :

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;1,2;3}(\underline{\rho},\underline{\sigma},\underline{x}):=\Phi_{1,2}\left(-\sigma_{1}x_{3}\right)=\Phi_{1,2}\left(-\sigma_{2}x_{4}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x_{1}x_{3}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x_{2}x_{3}\right)}{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\rho_{1}}x_{2}x_{3}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\rho_{2}}x_{1}x_{3}\right)},

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;1,2;3}(\underline{\rho},\underline{\sigma},\underline{x}):=\Phi_{1,2}\left(-\sigma_{1}x_{4}\right)=\Phi_{1,2}\left(-\sigma_{2}x_{3}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\rho_{1}}x_{1}x_{3}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x_{2}x_{3}\right)}{{\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\rho_{1}}x_{2}x_{3}\right){\theta_{q}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\rho_{2}}x_{1}x_{3}\right)}\cdot

We verify :

\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;1,2;3}(\underline{\rho},\underline{\sigma},\underline{x})=\mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;1,2;4}(\underline{\rho},\underline{\sigma},\underline{x})\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;1,2;3}(\underline{\rho},\underline{\sigma},\underline{x})=\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;1,2;4}(\underline{\rho},\underline{\sigma},\underline{x}).

Using the other decompositions we define similarly

\mathbf{e}_{q}^{h;i,j;k}(\underline{\rho},\underline{\sigma},\underline{x})

(where
h=1,2
and
(i,j,k)
is a set of
3
distinct elements of
\{1,2,3,4\}
).
We have :
\mathbf{e}_{q}^{h;i,j;k}=\mathbf{e}_{q}^{h;j,i;k}
and we verify :

\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;i,j;k}(\underline{\rho},\underline{\sigma},\underline{x})=\mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;i,j;l}(\underline{\rho},\underline{\sigma},\underline{x})\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;i,j;k}(\underline{\rho},\underline{\sigma},\underline{x})=\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;i,j;l}(\underline{\rho},\underline{\sigma},\underline{x}).

The function
\mathbf{e}_{q}^{h;i,j;k}
is elliptic in
\sigma_{h}
. We interpret the
12

functions as
q
-analogs of the
a_{l}=2\cos\theta_{l}
(
l=0,t,1,\infty
) of the
differential case (the traces of the local monodromies). A big difference is that
the
\mathbf{e}_{q}^{h;i,j;k}
involve
all
the local data.

We will call the
\mathbf{e}_{q}^{h;i,j;k}
the
q
-local monodromy invariants

. We conjecture that when the monodromy exponents

\underline{\rho},\penalty\ \underline{\sigma},\penalty\ \underline{x}
move these
q
-local monodromy
invariants “parameterize algebraically” the variation of

{\mathcal{F}}(\underline{\rho},\underline{\sigma},\underline{x})
.

We have :

\displaystyle(\Pi^{\prime})_{1,2}^{-1}(0)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{1,2}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;1,2;3}\right),\penalty\ \penalty\ (\Pi^{\prime})_{1,2}^{-1}(\infty)=\Pi_{1,2}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;1,2;3}\right),\penalty\ \penalty\

To the pairing
(\rho_{1}\leftrightarrow x_{i})

(
resp.

(\rho_{2}\leftrightarrow x_{i})\big)
we associate a line
L_{\rho_{1},\sigma_{i}}
(resp.

L_{\rho_{2},\sigma_{i}}
) of
{\mathcal{F}}
: it is the set of the classes of the matrices
M

such that the first (resp. second)
line
of
M(x_{i})
is null.
Similarly, to the pairing
(\sigma_{1}\leftrightarrow x_{i})

(resp.
(\sigma_{2}\leftrightarrow x_{i}))
we associate a line
L_{\sigma_{1},x_{i}}

(resp.
L_{\sigma_{2},x_{i}}
) of
{\mathcal{F}}
: it is the set defined by the classes of
the matrices
M
such that the first (resp. second)
column
of
M(x_{i})

is null. Using these lines we can describe the exceptional non logarithmic fibers
of
\Pi_{i,j}
(and
\Pi^{\prime}_{i,j}
). We detail the case
(i,j)=(1,2)
; the others
are similar.

We denote
\overline{M}\in{\mathcal{F}}
the equivalence class of
M\in F
. We have :

f_{i}=0\Leftrightarrow\overline{M}\in L_{\rho_{1},x_{i}}\Leftrightarrow M(x_{i})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\ast&\ast\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{i}=0\Leftrightarrow\overline{M}\in L_{\rho_{2},x_{i}}\Leftrightarrow M(x_{i})=\begin{pmatrix}\ast&\ast\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}.

Then (
cf.
theorem
5.5
) :

\Pi_{1,2}^{-1}(0)=\{f_{1}=0\}\cup\{g_{2}=0\}=L_{\rho_{1},x_{1}}\cup L_{\rho_{2},x_{2}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Pi_{1,2}^{-1}(\infty)=\{f_{2}=0\}\cup\{g_{1}=0\}=L_{\rho_{1},x_{2}}\cup L_{\rho_{2},x_{1}}.

f_{i}=0\Leftrightarrow\overline{M}\in L_{\rho_{1},x_{i}}\Leftrightarrow M(x_{i})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\ast&\ast\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{i}=0\Leftrightarrow\overline{M}\in L_{\rho_{2},x_{i}}\Leftrightarrow M(x_{i})=\begin{pmatrix}\ast&\ast\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}.

Similarly we have :

f^{\prime}_{i}=0\Leftrightarrow\overline{M}\in L_{\sigma_{1},x_{i}}\Leftrightarrow M(x_{i})=\begin{pmatrix}0&\ast\\
0&\ast\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad g^{\prime}_{i}=0\Leftrightarrow\overline{M}\in L_{\sigma_{2},x_{i}}\Leftrightarrow M(x_{i})=\begin{pmatrix}\ast&0\\
\ast&0\end{pmatrix}.

Then :

({\Pi^{\prime}})_{1,2}^{-1}(0)=\{f^{\prime}_{3}=0\}\cup\{g^{\prime}_{4}=0\}=L_{\sigma_{1},x_{3}}\cup L_{\sigma_{2},x_{4}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ ({\Pi^{\prime}})_{1,2}^{-1}(\infty)=\{f^{\prime}_{4}=0\}\cup\{g^{\prime}_{3}=0\}=L_{\sigma_{1},x_{4}}\cup L_{\sigma_{2},x_{3}}.

The two by two intersections of the
4
special fibers are empty, therefore
the
8
lines are distinct and we have for these lines the following incidence
relations :

(7.10.2)

\begin{split}L_{\rho_{1},x_{1}}\cap L_{\rho_{1},x_{2}}=\varnothing,\penalty\ L_{\rho_{2},x_{1}}&\cap L_{\rho_{2},x_{2}}=\varnothing,\penalty\ \penalty\ L_{\sigma_{1},x_{3}}\cap L_{\sigma_{1},x_{4}}=\varnothing,\penalty\ L_{\sigma_{2},x_{3}}\cap L_{\sigma_{2},x_{4}}=\varnothing,\\
\forall\,h,h^{\prime}=1,2,\penalty\ &\forall\,i=1,2,\penalty\ \forall\,j=3,4,\penalty\ \penalty\ L_{\rho_{h},x_{i}}\cap L_{\sigma_{h^{\prime}},x_{j}}=\varnothing\\
L_{\rho_{1},x_{1}}\cap L_{\rho_{2},x_{2}}&=\{\text{one\penalty\ point}\},\penalty\ \penalty\ L_{\rho_{1},x_{2}}\cap L_{\rho_{2},x_{1}}=\{\text{one\penalty\ point}\},\\
L_{\sigma_{1},x_{3}}\cap L_{\sigma_{2},x_{4}}&=\{\text{one\penalty\ point}\},\penalty\ \penalty\ L_{\sigma_{2},x_{3}}\cap L_{\sigma_{1},x_{4}}=\{\text{one\penalty\ point}\}.\end{split}

Replacing the decomposition
(1,2)
by another decomposition, we get similar results.

We cannot have

M(x_{i})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\ast&0\end{pmatrix}
,

M(x_{i})=\begin{pmatrix}\ast&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
,

M(x_{i})=\begin{pmatrix}0&\ast\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
,

M(x_{i})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\ast\end{pmatrix}
,
therefore :

\forall\,h,h^{\prime}=1,2,\penalty\ i=1,2,3,4,\quad L_{\rho_{h},x_{i}}\cap L_{\sigma_{h^{\prime}},x_{i}}=\varnothing.

For
h,h^{\prime}=1,2
and
i,j
fixed,
i\neq j
, the lines
L_{\rho_{h},x_{i}}
and

L_{\sigma_{h}^{\prime},x_{j}}
appear in two different exceptionnal fibers

\Pi_{i,k}^{-1}
(
k\neq i
and
k\neq j
), therefore

L_{\rho_{h},x_{i}}\cap L_{\sigma_{h}^{\prime},x_{j}}=\varnothing
.

Putting things together, we verify that we have
16

different
lines
with the following incidence relations :

—

for
h,h^{\prime}=1,2
,
i,j=1,2,3,4
,
L_{\rho_{h},x_{i}}\cap L_{\sigma_{h^{\prime}},x_{j}}=\varnothing
.

—

for

, if

then

L_{\rho_{h},x_{i}}\cap L_{\rho_{h},x_{j}}=\varnothing
;

—

for
h=1,2
, if
i\neq j
then

L_{\sigma_{h},x_{i}}\cap L_{\sigma_{h},x_{j}}=\varnothing
;

—

if
(i,j,k,l)
is a permutation of
(1,2,3,4)
, then :

—

L_{\rho_{1},x_{i}}
meets
L_{\rho_{2},x_{j}}
,

L_{\rho_{2},x_{k}}
,
L_{\rho_{2},x_{l}}
and the
3

distinct
;

—

L_{\rho_{2},x_{i}}
meets
L_{\rho_{1},x_{j}}
,

L_{\rho_{1},x_{k}}
,
L_{\rho_{1},x_{l}}
and the
3
intersection points are
distinct

—

if
(i,j,k,l)
is a permutation of
(1,2,3,4)
, then :

—

L_{\sigma_{1},x_{i}}
meets
L_{\sigma_{2},x_{j}}
,

L_{\sigma_{2},x_{k}}
,
L_{\sigma_{2},x_{l}}
and the
3

;

—

,

L_{\sigma_{1},x_{k}}
,
L_{\sigma_{1},x_{l}}
and the
3

distinct
.

Each line is contained into exactly
3
special fibers :

(7.10.3)

\begin{split}L_{\rho_{1},x_{i}}&\subset\Pi_{i,j}^{-1}(0),\penalty\ \penalty\ L_{\rho_{1},x_{i}}\subset\Pi_{i,k}^{-1}(0),\penalty\ \penalty\ L_{\rho_{1},x_{i}}\subset\Pi_{i,l}^{-1}(0)\\
L_{\rho_{2},x_{i}}&\subset\Pi_{i,j}^{-1}(\infty),\penalty\ \penalty\ L_{\rho_{2},x_{i}}\subset\Pi_{i,k}^{-1}(\infty),\penalty\ \penalty\ L_{\rho_{2},x_{i}}\subset\Pi_{i,l}^{-1}(\infty).\\
L_{\sigma_{1},x_{i}}&\subset\Pi_{j,k}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;j,k;i}),\penalty\ \penalty\ L_{\sigma_{1},x_{i}}\subset\Pi_{k,l}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;k,l;i}),\penalty\ \penalty\ L_{\sigma_{1},x_{i}}\subset\Pi_{l,j}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;l,j;i})\\
L_{\sigma_{2},x_{i}}&\subset\Pi_{j,k}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;j,k;i}),\penalty\ \penalty\ L_{\sigma_{2},x_{i}}\subset\Pi_{k,l}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;k,l;i}),\penalty\ \penalty\ L_{\sigma_{2},x_{i}}\subset\Pi_{l,j}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;l,j;i}).\end{split}

Moreover this line is equal to each pairwise intersection of the
3
special fibers.

If
(i,j,k,l)
is a permutation of
(1,2,3,4)
, then we have :

(7.10.4)

\begin{split}L_{\rho_{1},x_{i}}&=\Pi_{i,j}^{-1}(0)\cap\Pi_{i,k}^{-1}(0)=\Pi_{i,k}^{-1}(0)\cap\Pi_{i,l}^{-1}(0)=\Pi_{i,l}^{-1}(0)\cap\Pi_{i,j}^{-1}(0)\\
L_{\rho_{2},x_{i}}&=\Pi_{i,j}^{-1}(\infty)\cap\Pi_{i,k}^{-1}(\infty)=\Pi_{i,k}^{-1}(\infty)\cap\Pi_{i,l}^{-1}(\infty)=\Pi_{i,l}^{-1}(\infty)\cap\Pi_{i,j}^{-1}(\infty)\\
L_{\sigma_{1},x_{i}}&=\Pi_{j,k}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;j,k;i})\cap\Pi_{k,l}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;k,l;i})=\Pi_{k,l}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;k,l;i})\cap\Pi_{l,j}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;l,j;i})\\
&=\Pi_{l,j}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;l,j;i})\cap\Pi_{j,k}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;j,k;i})\\
L_{\sigma_{2},x_{i}}&=\Pi_{j,k}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;j,k;i})\cap\Pi_{k,l}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;k,l;i})=\Pi_{k,l}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;k,l;i})\cap\Pi_{l,j}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;l,j;i})\\
&=\Pi_{l,j}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;l,j;i})\cap\Pi_{j,k}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;j,k;i})\end{split}

We recall the notation :

\mathcal{U}_{i,j}={\mathcal{F}}\setminus\left(\Pi_{i,j}^{-1}(0)\cup\Pi_{i,j}^{-1}(\infty)\cup(\Pi^{\prime})_{i,j}^{-1}(0)\cup(\Pi^{\prime})_{i,j}^{-1}(\infty)\right)
.

Proposition 7.11

(i)

Each line intersects
3
other lines at
3
different points.

(ii)

If
a\in{\mathcal{F}}
, there are at most two different lines passing by
a
.

(iii)

The set
\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{i,j}
contains
{\mathcal{F}}
less all the logarithmic fibers.

(iv)

The set of
16
lines has two connected components: the set

\{L_{\rho_{h},x_{i}}\}_{h=1,2,;i=1,2,3,4}
and the set

\{L_{\sigma_{h},x_{i}}\}_{h=1,2,;i=1,2,3,4}
.

Proof. -

(i)

and  (ii) follows easily from the above relations

(iii)

Let
a\in{\mathcal{F}}
which does not belong to a logarithmic fiber. We suppose that

a\notin\mathcal{U}_{1,2}\cup\mathcal{U}_{1,3}\cup\mathcal{U}_{1,4}
. Then we have
in particular
a\notin\mathcal{U}_{1,2}
and
a
must belong to one of the
4

exceptional fibers and therefore to one of the eight lines. We have a similar
result for
(1,3)
and
(1,4)
, therefore
a
belongs to one of the four lines

L_{\rho_{h},x_{1}}
,
L_{\sigma_{h^{\prime}},x_{1}}
.

We prove similarly that
a
belongs to one of the four lines
L_{\rho_{h},x_{2}}
,

L_{\sigma_{h},x_{2}}
, to one of the four lines
L_{\rho_{h},x_{3}}
,
L_{\sigma_{h^{\prime}},x_{3}}
and
to one of the four lines
L_{\rho_{h},x_{4}}
,
L_{\sigma_{h^{\prime}},x_{4}}
. Then
a
belongs to

4
distinct lines. This contradicts (ii).

(iv)

The intersection of the two sets is empty and, using (i), we can verify that each
set is connected.

\Box

If conjecture
6.8
is true, then the above proposition
implies that
{\mathcal{F}}
is smooth, that is conjecture
7.10
.

7.2.6
An image of
{\mathcal{F}}
in
\left({{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}\right)^{3}

The image and what we know about it.

We consider the map

A better notation would be
T_{1,2,3,4}
: note that
T_{1,2,3,4}\neq T_{1,2,4,3}
because
there is an exchange between

and

We would like to describe the Zariski closure
59
59

59

is closed …

Y
in

Definition 7.12

The
(1,2)
-skeleton
of
{\mathcal{F}}

If

is a

(1,2,3,4)
we can define similarly
the
(i,j)

\text{Sk}_{(i,j)}({\mathcal{F}})

It is important to understand the skeleton structure and to describe the inclusion

\text{Sk}_{1,2}({\mathcal{F}})\subset T_{1,2}({\mathcal{F}})
. In the next paragraph we will explain how
(under some “reasonable” conjectures) it is possible to “sew the surface onto the
skeleton bones”.

We have
L_{\rho_{1},x_{2}}=\Pi_{1,2}^{-1}(0)\cap\Pi_{2,3}^{-1}(0)
, therefore the image of
this line in
\left({{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}\right)^{3}

\{u=v=0\}
, the intersection
of the planes
\{u=0\}
and
\{v=0\}

\{u=v=0\}
and therefore it is
equal to
\{u=v=0\}
punctured at a point. Its closure is the projective line.

We have similar results for the lines :

(7.12.1)

L_{\rho_{2},x_{2}},\penalty\ L_{\rho_{1},x_{3}},\penalty\ L_{\rho_{2},x_{3}},\penalty\ L_{\sigma_{1},x_{1}},\penalty\ L_{\sigma_{2},x_{1}},L_{\sigma_{1},x_{4}},\penalty\ L_{\sigma_{2},x_{4}}.

We will call

An important point is that it is possible to

We use

We can describe the closed half-skeleton using some pairs of plane equations

\begin{split}&\{u=0\},\quad\{u=\infty\},\quad\{u=\mathbf{e}_{q}^{1;1,2;3}\},\quad\{u=\mathbf{e}_{q}^{2;1,2;3}\}\\

Conjecture 7.14

The lines of the half-skeleton are double curves.

More precisely, in a neighborhood of a smooth point of the double curve, we have in local
coordinates
Y=\{xy=0\}
(mild singularity). There could also exist pinch points (in

complicated singular points.

The image of the half-skeleton

This image depends only on the four complex numbers

We recall the following definition.

Definition 7.15

Let
V
,
W
two complex algebraic varieties. A morphism
f:V\rightarrow W
is
a
branched covering
if the two dimensions are the same and if the typical
fiber of
f
is of dimension
0
.

There is a Zariski dense open set
W^{\prime}\subset W
such that
f
is unramified above
W^{\prime}

(a classical covering space). The complement of the largest possible
W^{\prime}
is called
the
branching locus
. If
W^{\prime}
is connected then the cardinal of the fiber is
constant, it is the
degree
of the branched covering.

Be careful, the classical notion of ramified covering is more restrictive:
all
the fibers
are finite sets.

Conjecture 7.16

The map
\pi_{2}
induced by the restriction to

\left({{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}\right)^{3}\rightarrow\left({{\mathbf{P}}^{1}({\mathbf{C}})}\right)^{2}

).

We will see later (

a projective completion of
{\mathcal{F}}
.

Another heuristic description of the image.

(i)

60

60
